I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` machines with powers `a_i`
2. We can perform at most one operation: choose two machines `i` and `j`, and an integer `x` such that:
   - `x` divides `a_i`
   - We reduce `a_i` by `x` times: `a_i = a_i / x`
   - We increase `a_j` by `x` times: `a_j = a_j * x`
   - All powers must remain positive integers
3. We want to find the minimum possible total power after any number of such operations (0 or 1)

## Key Insights

1. The total power changes in the operation: 
   - Original total: `sum(a_i)`
   - After operation: `(a_i - x) + (a_j + x) = a_i + a_j`
   - So the total power remains unchanged! `(a_i/x) + (a_j*x) = a_i + a_j` in terms of the sum
   - Wait, let me recalculate: `(a_i/x) + (a_j*x)` vs `a_i + a_j`
   - Actually: `a_i/x + a_j*x` vs `a_i + a_j`
   - The change is: `(a_i/x + a_j*x) - (a_i + a_j) = a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j = a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)`
   - This means the total power can actually change!

2. Let me reconsider: we're replacing `a_i` with `a_i/x` and `a_j` with `a_j*x`
   - New total = `(sum of all a_k) - a_i + (a_i/x) + (a_j*x) - a_j`
   - = `(sum of all a_k) + a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)`
   - = `(sum of all a_k) + a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)`

3. The total power change depends on the values of `a_i`, `a_j`, and `x`.

4. Since `x` must divide `a_i`, we can only choose divisors of `a_i`.

5. We want to minimize the total power, so we need to find the minimum value of the expression above over all valid choices of `i`, `j`, and divisors `x` of `a_i`.

6. Since we can also choose not to perform any operation, the answer is at least the original sum.

7. For each pair `(i, j)` and each divisor `x` of `a_i`, we can compute the new total power and take the minimum.

However, note that we only have up to 50,000 machines, and each `a_i` is at most 100. The number of divisors of any number ≤ 100 is at most 12 (for perfect numbers like 60).

But checking all pairs and all divisors might be expensive: O(n² × d) where d is the max number of divisors.

Let me check the constraints: n ≤ 50,000, but each a_i ≤ 100. The total number of pairs is about 2.5e9, which is too many.

We need a smarter approach.

## Key Observation

Let's look at the change in total power:
- Original total: `T = sum(a_i)`
- New total: `T' = T - a_i - a_j + (a_i/x) + (a_j * x)`

So the change is: `Δ = (a_i/x - a_i) + (a_j * x - a_j) = a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)`

We want to minimize `T' = T + Δ`.

Since `T` is fixed, minimizing `T'` is equivalent to minimizing `Δ`.

So we need to find the minimum value of `a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)` over all valid choices.

Note that `1/x - 1` is negative for all `x ≥ 1`, and `x - 1` is non-negative for `x ≥ 1`.

So `a_i(1/x - 1)` is negative, while `a_j(x - 1)` is non-negative. For small `x`, the first term is more negative (since `1/x` is larger), but the second term is smaller.

Let me try with specific values:

For a fixed `a_i`, as `x` increases:
- `1/x - 1` becomes more negative
- `x - 1` becomes more positive

The net effect depends on which dominates.

But since `x` must divide `a_i`, we only consider divisors of `a_i`.

For each machine `i`, we can consider all its divisors `x`, and for each such `x`, we can try all machines `j` and compute the change.

But this is still O(n² × d) which is too slow.

Wait - let me think again. The minimum total power might be achieved when `x = 1` (no change), or when `x` is small.

Actually, let's consider what happens when `x = 1`:
- Change = `a_i(1/1 - 1) + a_j(1 - 1) = 0`
- So total power stays the same.

When `x > 1`, `1/x - 1` is negative, so `a_i(1/x - 1)` is negative, which reduces the total power.

The term `a_j(x - 1)` is non-negative, so it increases the total power.

But if `a_i(1/x - 1)` is large negative (when `x` is small), and `a_j(x - 1)` is small (when `x` is small), it might be beneficial.

For any `x > 1`, `x - 1 ≥ 1`, so `a_j(x - 1) ≥ a_j`.

For the first term: `a_i(1/x - 1) = a_i(1/x - 1) = -a_i(1 - 1/x)`

So the total change is: `-a_i(1 - 1/x) + a_j(x - 1)`

We want to minimize this (make it as negative as possible).

For a fixed `a_i`, as `x` increases, `1 - 1/x` increases (so the negative term becomes more negative), and `x - 1` increases. 

But both terms increase as `x` increases.

Let me try with a simple example: `a_i = 4`, `a_j = 5`, `x = 2`:
- Change = -4(1 - 1/2) + 5(2 - 1) = -4(0.5) + 5(1) = -2 + 5 = 3

With `x = 4`:
- Change = -4(1 - 1/4) + 5(4 - 1) = -4(0.75) + 5(3) = -3 + 15 = 12

So the change increases as `x` increases.

For `x = 2`: change = 3
For `x = 1`: change = 0

So the minimum change for this pair is 0 (when `x = 1`).

When does the change become negative?

We need: `-a_i(1 - 1/x) + a_j(x - 1) < 0`

Which means: `a_j(x - 1) < a_i(1 - 1/x)`

Since `1 - 1/x > 0`, we have `a_j(x - 1) < a_i(1 - 1/x)`

But `a_j(x - 1)` grows with `x`, and `a_i(1 - 1/x)` approaches `a_i`.

So for large `x`, the left side grows linearly, right side approaches `a_i`.

Therefore, for large enough `x`, the inequality can't hold.

The minimum change might occur at small `x` values.

Actually, let me try with `a_i = 4`, `a_j = 1`:
- `x = 2`: change = -4(1 - 1/2) + 1(2 - 1) = -4(0.5) + 1(1) = -2 + 1 = -1
- `x = 4`: change = -4(1 - 1/4) + 1(4 - 1) = -4(0.75) + 3 = -3 + 3 = 0

So `x = 2` gives a negative change of -1, which reduces total power by 1.

This is possible! So the total power can actually decrease.

Back to the algorithm:

We need to check all pairs (i, j) and all divisors x of a_i, and compute the new total power.

But is O(n² × d) acceptable? n ≤ 50,000, d ≤ 12 (since max value is 100), so n² × d ≤ 50,000² × 12 = 25e9 × 12 = 300e9 which is way too slow.

We need to optimize.

What if we fix i and consider all divisors of a_i, and for each divisor x, we want to find the j that minimizes the change.

The change is: `-a_i(1 - 1/x) + a_j(x - 1)`

We want to minimize this, which means for a fixed i and x, we want to minimize `a_j(x - 1) - a_i(1 - 1/x)`.

Since we want the minimum value, we need to minimize `a_j(x - 1)` (because `-a_i(1 - 1/x)` is constant for fixed i,x).

So for fixed i and x, the best j is the one with minimum `a_j`.

But wait, what if we want to minimize the overall expression? For fixed i and x, we need to minimize `a_j(x - 1)`.

Since `x - 1 ≥ 0`, if `x = 1`, then `x - 1 = 0`, so this term is 0 regardless of a_j.

If `x > 1`, then `x - 1 > 0`, so we should choose the machine j with the smallest `a_j`.

However, we can't choose j = i, because we are reducing a_i and increasing a_j.

The problem says "one machine and increase the power of one another machine", so j ≠ i.

So j can be any other machine.

Therefore, for each i and each divisor x of a_i (x ≥ 1), the best j is:
- If x = 1: any j, change is 0
- If x > 1: choose the machine j ≠ i with minimum a_j

But we need to consider that the term `-a_i(1 - 1/x)` is negative, so when we have a very small a_j, the positive term `a_j(x-1)` might be small.

However, we are trying to minimize the total change, so we want the entire expression to be as small (as negative as possible) as possible.

For fixed i and x > 1, the change is: `-a_i(1 - 1/x) + a_j(x - 1)`

To minimize this, since `-a_i(1 - 1/x)` is fixed for fixed i,x, we need to minimize `a_j(x - 1)`.

Since `x - 1 > 0`, this means we want the smallest `a_j` for j ≠ i.

Therefore, for each i and each divisor x of a_i (x ≥ 1), we can compute:
- If x = 1: change = 0
- If x > 1: change = `-a_i(1 - 1/x) + min{a_j | j ≠ i} * (x - 1)`

But we can precompute the minimum value of a_j for all j ≠ i.

Actually, we can precompute the global minimum, but we need to exclude the current machine.

So let's precompute the minimum value of a_j for all j, and also the second minimum.

But actually, for each machine i, we need min_{j ≠ i} a_j.

We can precompute this in O(n) time.

So algorithm:
1. Read input
2. Compute original total sum
3. Precompute `min_val[i]` = minimum value among all a_j where j ≠ i
4. For each machine i from 0 to n-1:
   - For each divisor x of a_i (including x=1):
     - If x = 1: change = 0
     - If x > 1: 
       - compute change = `-a_i*(1 - 1/x) + min_val[i] * (x - 1)`
       - update the minimum change seen so far
5. Answer = original_sum + min_change

But we also have the option of doing nothing (change = 0), so minimum change is at least 0.

Wait, in the example: [1, 2, 3, 4, 5], output is 14.

Original sum = 1+2+3+4+5 = 15.

So the minimum total power is 14, meaning the change is -1.

Let me verify with the example note:
- Reduce 4th machine (value 4) by 2 times: 4/2 = 2
- Increase 1st machine (value 1) by 2 times: 1*2 = 2
- New array: [2, 2, 3, 2, 5], sum = 2+2+3+2+5 = 14

Change: 14 - 15 = -1.

In my formula, for i=3 (0-indexed, a_i=4), x=2:
- change = -4*(1 - 1/2) + min_val[3] * (2 - 1)
- min_val[3] is the minimum a_j for j ≠ 3
- Original array: [1,2,3,4,5], so min_val[3] = min(1,2,3,5) = 1
- change = -4*(0.5) + 1*(1) = -2 + 1 = -1

Perfect!

So the algorithm is correct.

Now, about time complexity:
- For each i: we iterate over divisors of a_i. Since a_i ≤ 100, and the number of divisors of any number ≤ 100 is at most 12.
- So total operations: n × 12 = 50,000 × 12 = 600,000, which is acceptable.

Steps:
1. Read n and the array a
2. Compute total_sum = sum(a)
3. Precompute min_val[i] for each i: the minimum value in the array excluding position i
4. For each i from 0 to n-1:
   - Find all divisors of a[i]
   - For each divisor x of a[i]:
     - If x == 1: change = 0
     - Else: change = -a[i]*(1 - 1/x) + min_val[i] * (x - 1)
     - Update min_change = min(min_change, change)
5. Answer = total_sum + min_change

We need to be careful with floating point. We can avoid floating point by using fractions.

- -a[i]*(1 - 1/x) = -a[i]*( (x-1)/x ) = -a[i]*(x-1)/x

So change = -a[i]*(x-1)/x + min_val[i]*(x-1) = (x-1) * (min_val[i] - a[i]/x)

To avoid floating point, we can write:
change = (x-1) * (min_val[i] - a[i]/x)

But since we're dealing with integers, we can write as:
change = (x-1) * (min_val[i] * x - a[i]) / x

This is an integer only if x divides (min_val[i] * x - a[i]).

But min_val[i] * x - a[i] might not be divisible by x.

Wait: min_val[i] * x - a[i] is divisible by x only if x divides a[i], which it does (since x divides a[i]).

So yes, x divides a[i], so min_val[i] * x - a[i] is divisible by x.

So let's define: numerator = (x-1) * (min_val[i] * x - a[i])
denominator = x

Then change = numerator / x

Since x divides (min_val[i] * x - a[i]), x divides the numerator, so it's an integer.

But we don't need to compute it this way - we can just use floating point since a[i] ≤ 100, x ≤ 100, so the values are small and the division will be exact.

Alternatively, we can compute it as:
change = (x-1) * (min_val[i] - a[i]/x)

But a[i]/x might not be integer in floating point? Actually, since x divides a[i], a[i]/x is integer.

So we can compute:
change = (x-1) * (min_val[i] - a[i]//x)

Wait, no: min_val[i] - a[i]/x is not necessarily integer. For example, if min_val[i]=1, a[i]=4, x=2, then 1 - 4/2 = 1 - 2 = -1, which is integer.

Actually, since a[i]/x is integer, and min_val[i] is integer, their difference is integer.

And (x-1) is integer, so the product is integer.

So we can compute it safely.

But to avoid floating point issues, I'll use integer arithmetic.

change = (x-1) * (min_val[i] * x - a[i]) // x

Yes! This is exact.

So the algorithm in code:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_sum = sum(a)

# Precompute min_val[i] = minimum value among a[j] for j != i
min_val = []
for i in range(n):
    # Find minimum of all elements except a[i]
    current_min = a[0]
    for j in range(1, n):
        if j != i and a[j] < current_min:
            current_min = a[j]
    min_val.append(current_min)

# Find the minimum possible change
min_change = 0  # at least we can do nothing

for i in range(n):
    # Get all divisors of a[i]
    divisors = []
    x = 1
    while x * x <= a[i]:
        if a[i] % x == 0:
            divisors.append(x)
            if x != a[i] // x:
                divisors.append(a[i] // x)
        x += 1
    
    # For each divisor x of a[i]
    for x in divisors:
        if x == 1:
            change = 0
        else:
            # change = (x-1) * (min_val[i] * x - a[i]) // x
            numerator = (x - 1) * (min_val[i] * x - a[i])
            change = numerator // x
            # Since x divides (min_val[i]*x - a[i]), this is an integer
        if change < min_change:
            min_change = change

answer = total_sum + min_change
print(answer)
```

Let me verify with the examples.

Example 1: [1,2,3,4,5]
total_sum = 15
We need min_change = -1

For i=3 (a[i]=4), divisors of 4: [1,2,4]
- x=1: change=0
- x=2: change = (2-1)*(1*2 - 4)//2 = 1*(2-4)//2 = (-2)//2 = -1
- x=4: change = (4-1)*(1*4 - 4)//4 = 3*(0)//4 = 0

So min_change = -1, answer = 15 + (-1) = 14. Correct.

Example 2: [4,2,4,4]
total_sum = 4+2+4+4 = 14
Output should be 14.

Let's check if we can get a negative change.

For any machine:
- If we pick a machine with value 4, divisors: [1,2,4]
- For x=2: change = (2-1)*(min_val[i]*2 - 4)//2

min_val[i] depends on i:
- i=0 (a[0]=4): min_val[0] = min(2,4,4) = 2
  - x=2: (1)*(2*2 - 4)//2 = (4-4)//2 = 0
- i=1 (a[1]=2): divisors of 2: [1,2]
  - x=2: min_val[1] = min(4,4,4) = 4
    - change = (1)*(4*2 - 2)//2 = (8-2)//2 = 6//2 = 3
- i=2: similar to i=0, min_val[2]=2 (from element 2)
  - x=2: (1)*(2*2 - 4)//2 = 0
- i=3: similar to i=0, min_val[3]=2, x=2: 0

So minimum change is 0, so answer = 14. Correct.

Example 3: [2,4,2,3,7]
total_sum = 2+4+2+3+7 = 18
Output should be 18.

Check if any operation gives negative change.

For each i, try x>1 divisors.

i=0 (a[0]=2): divisors [1,2]
- x=1: 0
- x=2: min_val[0] = min(4,2,3,7) = 2
  - change = (2-1)*(2*2 - 2)//2 = 1*(4-2)//2 = 2//2 = 1 > 0

i=1 (a[1]=4): divisors [1,2,4]
- x=2: min_val[1] = min(2,2,3,7) = 2
  - change = (1)*(2*2 - 4)//2 = (4-4)//2 = 0
- x=4: min_val[1] = 2
  - change = (3)*(2*4 - 4)//4 = 3*(8-4)//4 = 12//4 = 3

i=2 (a[2]=2): same as i=0
- x=2: change = 1

i=3 (a[3]=3): divisors [1,3]
- x=3: min_val[3] = min(2,4,2,7) = 2
  - change = (3-1)*(2*3 - 3)//3 = 2*(6-3)//3 = 6//3 = 2

i=4 (a[4]=7): divisors [1,7]
- x=7: min_val[4] = min(2,4,2,3) = 2
  - change = (6)*(2*7 - 7)//7 = 6*(14-7)//7 = 42//7 = 6

So minimum change is 0, answer = 18. Correct.

Perfect!

Edge case: when n=2, and a=[1,1], total_sum=2.
For i=0, a[0]=1, divisors: [1]
- Only x=1, change=0
For i=1, same.
So answer=2.

Another case: [3,1], total_sum=4.
i=0: a[0]=3, divisors [1,3]
- x=1: 0
- x=3: min_val[0] = min(1) = 1
  - change = (3-1)*(1*3 - 3)//3 = 2*(0)//3 = 0
So answer=4.

Another case: [4,1], total_sum=5
i=0: a[0]=4, divisors [1,2,4]
- x=2: min_val[0] = 1
  - change = (1)*(1*2 - 4)//2 = (2-4)//2 = (-2)//2 = -1
- x=4: change = (3)*(1*4 - 4)//4 = 3*0//4 = 0
So min_change = -1, answer = 5 + (-1) = 4.

Verify: reduce a[0]=4 by 2: 4/2=2, increase a[1]=1 by 2: 1*2=2, new array [2,2], sum=4. Correct.

Final code:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total_sum = sum(a)

# Precompute min_val[i] = minimum value among a[j] for j != i
min_val = []
for i in range(n):
    current_min = a[0]
    for j in range(1, n):
        if j != i and a[j] < current_min:
            current_min = a[j]
    min_val.append(current_min)

# Find the minimum possible change
min_change = 0  # at least we can do nothing

for i in range(n):
    # Get all divisors of a[i]
    divisors = []
    x = 1
    while x * x <= a[i]:
        if a[i] % x == 0:
            divisors.append(x)
            if x != a[i] // x:
                divisors.append(a[i] // x)
        x += 1
    
    # For each divisor x of a[i]
    for x in divisors:
        if x == 1:
            change = 0
        else:
            # change = (x-1) * (min_val[i] * x - a[i]) // x
            numerator = (x - 1) * (min_val[i] * x - a[i])
            change = numerator // x
        if change < min_change:
            min_change = change

answer = total_sum + min_change
print(answer)
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

One day Sasha visited the farmer 2D and his famous magnetic farm. On this farm, the crop grows due to the influence of a special magnetic field. Maintaining of the magnetic field is provided by $n$ machines, and the power of the $i$-th machine is $a_i$. 

This year 2D decided to cultivate a new culture, but what exactly he didn't say. For the successful growth of the new culture, it is necessary to slightly change the powers of the machines. 2D can at most once choose an arbitrary integer $x$, then choose one machine and reduce the power of its machine by $x$ times, and at the same time increase the power of one another machine by $x$ times (powers of all the machines must stay positive integers). Note that he may not do that if he wants. More formally, 2D can choose two such indices $i$ and $j$, and one integer $x$ such that $x$ is a divisor of $a_i$, and change powers as following: $a_i = \frac{a_i}{x}$, $a_j = a_j \cdot x$

Sasha is very curious, that's why he wants to calculate the minimum total power the farmer can reach. There are too many machines, and Sasha can't cope with computations, help him!


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer $n$ ($2 \le n \le 5 \cdot 10^4$) — the number of machines.

The second line contains $n$ integers $a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n$ ($1 \le a_i \le 100$) — the powers of the machines.


-----Output-----

Print one integer — minimum total power.


-----Examples-----
Input
5
1 2 3 4 5

Output
14

Input
4
4 2 4 4

Output
14

Input
5
2 4 2 3 7

Output
18



-----Note-----

In the first example, the farmer can reduce the power of the $4$-th machine by $2$ times, and increase the power of the $1$-st machine by $2$ times, then the powers will be: $[2, 2, 3, 2, 5]$.

In the second example, the farmer can reduce the power of the $3$-rd machine by $2$ times, and increase the power of the $2$-nd machine by $2$ times. At the same time, the farmer can leave is be as it is and the total power won't change.

In the third example, it is optimal to leave it be as it is.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` machines with powers `a_i`
2. We can perform at most one operation: choose two machines `i` and `j`, and an integer `x` such that `x` divides `a_i`
3. The operation changes: `a_i = a_i / x` and `a_j = a_j * x`
4. All powers must remain positive integers
5. We want to find the minimum possible total power

## Key Insights

1. The total power sum changes when we perform the operation:
   - Before: `sum(a_i)`
   - After: `sum(a_i) - x + x = sum(a_i)` 
   - Wait, this seems like the sum remains unchanged!

Let me verify: original sum is `S`. After operation: `(a_i - x) + (a_j + x) = a_i + a_j`. That's the same as before. So the total power sum doesn't change!

But looking at the examples:
- Example 1: [1,2,3,4,5] → sum = 15, output = 14
- Example 2: [4,2,4,4] → sum = 14, output = 14  
- Example 3: [2,4,2,3,7] → sum = 18, output = 18

Wait, this suggests the sum doesn't change. But example 1 shows output 14 while original sum is 15. That doesn't make sense.

Let me recheck the example 1: 
Original: [1,2,3,4,5] sum = 15
After operation: [2,2,3,2,5] sum = 2+2+3+2+5 = 14

Oh! The sum decreased from 15 to 14. That means my earlier analysis is wrong.

Let me recalculate: 
- Machine i (4th) had power 4, reduced by x=2 → becomes 4/2 = 2
- Machine j (1st) had power 1, increased by x=2 → becomes 1*2 = 2
- Change in sum: (2 - 4) + (2 - 1) = -2 + 1 = -1
- Total sum becomes 15 - 1 = 14

Ah! So the sum decreases by `x`. The new sum is `original_sum - x`.

Therefore, to minimize the total power, we want to maximize `x` (since we subtract `x` from the total sum).

But we have constraints:
- `x` must be a divisor of `a_i` (the machine we're reducing)
- `a_i / x` must be a positive integer (which it will be since x divides a_i)
- `a_j * x` must be a positive integer (which it will be)

So the strategy is:
1. We can choose any machine `i` and any divisor `x` of `a_i`
2. We can choose any other machine `j`
3. The new total power is `original_sum - x`
4. To minimize this, we want to maximize `x`

Therefore, we need to find the maximum possible `x` across all valid operations.

But we can't just take any divisor - we need to ensure the operation is valid.

The maximum possible value of `x` would be the maximum divisor of any `a_i` that we can use. Since we can choose any divisor, and we want to maximize `x`, we should look for the largest possible divisor of any machine's power.

However, we have to be careful: the operation reduces `a_i` to `a_i/x`, and increases `a_j` to `a_j*x`. As long as `a_i/x` is positive (it will be), and `a_j*x` is positive (it will be), it's valid.

Therefore, the maximum possible `x` we can achieve is the maximum value among all divisors of all `a_i` values.

But we can only use divisors that actually exist in the array. So we need the maximum divisor of any `a_i`.

Wait, the maximum divisor of a number `a_i` is `a_i` itself. So if we use `x = a_i`, then `a_i/x = 1`, and `a_j*x = a_j * a_i`.

So for any machine i, we can choose `x = a_i`, and the new sum becomes `original_sum - a_i`.

The total power after operation would be `original_sum - x`.

To minimize the total power, we want to maximize `x`.

The maximum possible `x` we can use is `max(a_i)`.

But is `x = max(a_i)` always valid? Yes, because for any machine with value `a_i`, since `x = a_i` divides `a_i`, we can use it. Then we reduce that machine's power from `a_i` to `1`, and increase some other machine's power by `a_i`.

So the minimum total power is `original_sum - max(a_i)`.

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: [1,2,3,4,5], sum = 15, max = 5, result = 15-5 = 10. But expected output is 14.

This doesn't match. What's wrong?

Wait, in the example, they say the result is 14, not 10. So my reasoning is still wrong.

Let me trace through the example again. They say: reduce 4th machine (power 4) by 2 times, so 4/2 = 2, and increase 1st machine (power 1) by 2 times, so 1*2 = 2. Final: [2,2,3,2,5], sum = 14.

So they used x=2, not x=4. Why didn't they use x=4?

If they used x=4, machine 4 had power 4, reduced to 4/4 = 1, and machine 1 (power 1) becomes 1*4 = 4. Final: [4,2,3,1,5], sum = 4+2+3+1+5 = 15.

Sum didn't decrease! That can't be.

Wait: original sum is 1+2+3+4+5 = 15.
After operation: if x=4, new powers: (1-4) + (4/4) + others = (1-4) + (1) = -3? That's not right.

I think I made an error in the algebra.

When we reduce machine i from `a_i` to `a_i/x`, and increase machine j from `a_j` to `a_j*x`, the change in sum is:
`(a_i/x - a_i) + (a_j*x - a_j) = a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)`

So the new sum is:
`original_sum + a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)`

This is not equal to `original_sum - x`.

Let me recalculate with the example:
i=4 (power 4), j=1 (power 1), x=2
Change: (4/2 - 4) + (1*2 - 1) = (2-4) + (2-1) = -2 + 1 = -1
New sum: 15 - 1 = 14 ✓

With x=4:
Change: (4/4 - 4) + (1*4 - 1) = (1-4) + (4-1) = -3 + 3 = 0
New sum: 15 + 0 = 15

So the change is `a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)` = `a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j`

So total power after operation: `original_sum + (a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j)`

We want to minimize this, so we want to minimize `(a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j)`.

Since `a_i/x` and `a_j*x` are always positive, we want this expression to be as negative as possible.

Let me define `f(x) = a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j = (a_i/x - a_j) + (a_j*x - a_i)`

Actually: `f(x) = a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j = (a_i/x + a_j*x) - (a_i + a_j)`

So we want to minimize `(a_i/x + a_j*x) - (a_i + a_j)`.

Since `(a_i + a_j)` is constant, we want to minimize `a_i/x + a_j*x`.

This is a function of x, and we need to find its minimum over all divisors x of `a_i`.

For a fixed pair (i,j), we want to minimize `a_i/x + a_j*x` over divisors x of `a_i`.

By AM-GM inequality: `a_i/x + a_j*x >= 2*sqrt(a_i/x * a_j*x) = 2*sqrt(a_i*a_j)`

Equality when `a_i/x = a_j*x`, i.e., `x^2 = a_i/a_j`, so `x = sqrt(a_i/a_j)`.

But x must be integer and divide `a_i`.

For each possible machine i (the one being reduced), and each divisor x of a_i, we can compute the change in sum: `a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j`, and then the new total power is `original_sum + (a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j)`.

We want the minimum possible total power across all valid operations and all machines.

Also, we can choose not to perform any operation, so the answer is at least `original_sum`.

So algorithm:
1. Calculate original sum
2. For each machine i:
   - For each divisor x of a_i:
     - For each other machine j:
       - Calculate change = a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j
       - New sum = original_sum + change
       - Track the minimum new sum
3. Answer is min(original_sum, min_new_sum)

But this is O(n * d_max * n) where d_max is max divisors, which could be up to 100, so max divisors is around 10 (since 100 has only 9 divisors).

For each number up to 100, the number of divisors is at most 12 (e.g., 60 has divisors: 1,2,3,4,5,6,10,12,15,20,30,60).

So total operations: n (up to 50000) * 12 (divisors) * n (j choices) = 50000 * 12 * 50000 = 30e9, which is too slow.

We need optimization.

Alternative approach:
For each machine i and each divisor x of a_i, we want to minimize `a_i/x + a_j*x` over j ≠ i.

For a fixed i and x, the term `a_j*x` is linear in a_j. To minimize `a_i/x + a_j*x`, we want to minimize `a_j*x`, which means we want the smallest `a_j` among all machines j ≠ i.

For each machine i and each divisor x of a_i, the minimum value of `a_j*x` over j ≠ i is `x * min(a_j for j ≠ i)`.

Therefore, for fixed i and x, we want to minimize `a_i/x + x * min_other_a_j`.

Then the change in sum is `a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j = (a_i/x + a_j*x) - (a_i + a_j)`.

For fixed i and x, the minimum over j is when we take the j with minimum a_j.

But wait, we need to find the j that minimizes `a_j*x`, which is when `a_j` is min among j ≠ i.

So for each i and each x | a_i, let `m = min(a_j for j ≠ i)`, then the minimum gain from the j choice is `m * x`.

Then the change in sum is `a_i/x - a_i + m*x - a_j_for_that_j`, but we don't have a specific j.

Actually, if we take the machine with minimum value (excluding i), then the change is:
`a_i/x - a_i + (m*x) - (m) = (a_i/x + m*x - m) - a_i`

Wait, no: when we replace a_j with a_j*x, we're adding (a_j*x - a_j) to the sum.

So for a specific j with value v, the change in sum is `a_i/x - a_i + v*x - v`.

We want to minimize this over all j ≠ i.

Since v appears linearly, the minimum occurs at the minimum possible v among j ≠ i.

So yes, for each i and divisor x of a_i, let `m_i = min(a_j for j ≠ i)`, then the change in sum is `a_i/x - a_i + m_i*x - m_i`.

Then new total sum = `original_sum + (a_i/x - a_i + m_i*x - m_i)`.

We need to compute this for all i and all divisors x of a_i, and take the minimum.

Then answer is min(original_sum, min_over_all_operations_of_new_sum)

Let me verify with example 1: [1,2,3,4,5]
original_sum = 15

For each i, compute m_i = min of others:
- i=0 (a_i=1): others = [2,3,4,5], m_i = 2
- i=1 (a_i=2): others = [1,3,4,5], m_i = 1  
- i=2 (a_i=3): others = [1,2,4,5], m_i = 1

- i=4 (a_i=5): others = [1,2,3,4], m_i = 1

For each i and divisor x of a_i:
i=0 (a_i=1): divisors of 1: [1]

  new_sum = 15 + 0 = 15

i=1 (a_i=2): divisors [1,2]

Minimum new sum is 15, so answer is 15.

But expected is 14. What's wrong?

So new sum = 15 - 1 = 14.

But according to my calculation with m_i=2, I used v=2, not v=1.

I see the mistake! When I said "the minimum v among j≠i", that gives the minimum value, but when we compute `v*x - v`, it's `-v + v*x`, which is `v*(x-1)`.

For fixed x and i, we want to minimize `v*(x-1)` over j≠i.

Since v is positive, if x>1, then x-1>0, so we want the minimum v.
If x=1, then x-1=0, so change is 0 regardless of v.

  x=2: change = 2/2 - 2 + 2*2 - 2 = 1-2 + 4-2 = -1 + 2 = 1
  new_sum = 19

For i=1: a_i=4, divisors [1,2,4]
  x=1: 0
  x=2: change = 4/2 - 4 + 2*2 - 2 = 2-4 + 4-2 = 0
  x=4: change = 4/4 - 4 + 2*4 - 2 = 1-4 + 8-2 = -3 + 6 = 3
  new_sum = 21

All others will be >= 0, so minimum is 18.

This matches.

Therefore, the algorithm is:

1. Read n and array a
2. Calculate original_sum = sum(a)
3. Precompute m[i] = min(a[j] for j != i) for each i
4. Initialize min_total = original_sum
5. For each i from 0 to n-1:
   - For each divisor x of a[i]:
     - Calculate change = a[i]/x - a[i] + m[i] * x - m[i]
     - new_sum = original_sum + change
     - min_total = min(min_total, new_sum)
6. Print min_total

Note: x must be a positive divisor of a[i], and since a[i] <= 100, we can iterate through possible divisors by checking from 1 to sqrt(a[i]).

Actually, since a[i] <= 100, we can simply iterate x from 1 to a[i], and check if a[i] % x == 0.

But to be efficient, we can iterate from 1 to int(sqrt(a[i])), and add both x and a[i]/x if they are different.

But since the constraint is small, we can just iterate from 1 to a[i].

The worst-case a[i] is 100, so 100 iterations per element, and n up to 50000, so 5e6 iterations, which is acceptable.

Let me implement:

```python
import math

n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

original_sum = sum(a)

# Precompute m[i] = minimum value among all j != i
m = [0] * n
for i in range(n):
    min_val = float('inf')
    for j in range(n):
        if i != j:
            min_val = min(min_val, a[j])
    m[i] = min_val

min_total = original_sum

# For each machine i, and each divisor x of a[i]
for i in range(n):
    # Get all divisors of a[i]
    divisors = []
    x = 1
    while x * x <= a[i]:
        if a[i] % x == 0:
            divisors.append(x)
            if x != a[i] // x:
                divisors.append(a[i] // x)
        x += 1
    
    # For each divisor x, calculate the change in sum
    for x in divisors:
        # The machine with minimum value among others (excluding i)
        min_other_val = m[i]
        # Change in sum: (a[i]/x - a[i]) + (min_other_val*x - min_other_val)
        change = (a[i] // x) - a[i] + min_other_val * (x - 1)
        new_sum = original_sum + change
        if new_sum < min_total:
            min_total = new_sum

print(min_total)
```

Wait, let me verify the change formula:
We have:
- Reduce machine i: from a[i] to a[i]/x, change = a[i]/x - a[i]
- Increase machine j (with value min_other_val): from min_other_val to min_other_val*x, change = min_other_val*x - min_other_val = min_other_val*(x-1)
- Total change = a[i]/x - a[i] + min_other_val*(x-1)

Yes, correct.

In the example i=3 in first example: a[i]=4, x=2, min_other_val=1
change = 4/2 - 4 + 1*(2-1) = 2-4 + 1 = -1
new_sum = 15 -1 = 14 ✓

Perfect.

Edge case: what if there are no valid operations? But since we can always choose not to operate, min_total >= original_sum.

Also, if a[i] = 1, divisors are only [1].

This should work.

Let me test with the examples.

Example 1: n=5, a=[1,2,3,4,5], original_sum=15
m[0]=2, m[1]=1, m[2]=1, m[3]=1, m[4]=1

For i=3 (a[i]=4), divisors: x=1,2,4
x=1: change = 4/1 - 4 + 1*(1-1) = 4-4 + 0 = 0
x=2: change = 2-4 + 1*(2-1) = -2 + 1 = -1
x=4: change = 1-4 + 1*(4-1) = -3 + 3 = 0
new_sum for x=2: 14

min_total becomes 14.

Yes.

Final code:

```python
import math

n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

original_sum = sum(a)

# Precompute m[i] = minimum value among all j != i
m = [0] * n
for i in range(n):
    min_val = float('inf')
    for j in range(n):
        if i != j:
            min_val = min(min_val, a[j])
    m[i] = min_val

min_total = original_sum

# For each machine i, and each divisor x of a[i]
for i in range(n):
    # Get all divisors of a[i]
    divisors = []
    x = 1
    while x * x <= a[i]:
        if a[i] % x == 0:
            divisors.append(x)
            if x != a[i] // x:
                divisors.append(a[i] // x)
        x += 1
    
    # For each divisor x, calculate the change in sum
    for x in divisors:
        # Change in sum: (a[i]/x - a[i]) + (m[i] * (x - 1))
        change = (a[i] // x) - a[i] + m[i] * (x - 1)
        new_sum = original_sum + change
        if new_sum < min_total:
            min_total = new_sum

print(min_total)
```

            min_val = min(min_val, a[j])

# For each machine i, and each divisor x of a[i]
for i in range(n):

    divisors = []

        if a[i] % x == 0:
            divisors.append(x)
            if x != a[i] // x:
                divisors.append(a[i] // x)

    # For each divisor x, calculate the change in sum

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

One day Sasha visited the farmer 2D and his famous magnetic farm. On this farm, the crop grows due to the influence of a special magnetic field. Maintaining of the magnetic field is provided by $n$ machines, and the power of the $i$-th machine is $a_i$. 

This year 2D decided to cultivate a new culture, but what exactly he didn't say. For the successful growth of the new culture, it is necessary to slightly change the powers of the machines. 2D can at most once choose an arbitrary integer $x$, then choose one machine and reduce the power of its machine by $x$ times, and at the same time increase the power of one another machine by $x$ times (powers of all the machines must stay positive integers). Note that he may not do that if he wants. More formally, 2D can choose two such indices $i$ and $j$, and one integer $x$ such that $x$ is a divisor of $a_i$, and change powers as following: $a_i = \frac{a_i}{x}$, $a_j = a_j \cdot x$

Sasha is very curious, that's why he wants to calculate the minimum total power the farmer can reach. There are too many machines, and Sasha can't cope with computations, help him!


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer $n$ ($2 \le n \le 5 \cdot 10^4$) — the number of machines.

The second line contains $n$ integers $a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n$ ($1 \le a_i \le 100$) — the powers of the machines.


-----Output-----

Print one integer — minimum total power.


-----Examples-----
Input
5
1 2 3 4 5

Output
14

Input
4
4 2 4 4

Output
14

Input
5
2 4 2 3 7

Output
18

In the first example, the farmer can reduce the power of the $4$-th machine by $2$ times, and increase the power of the $1$-st machine by $2$ times, then the powers will be: $[2, 2, 3, 2, 5]$.

In the second example, the farmer can reduce the power of the $3$-rd machine by $2$ times, and increase the power of the $2$-nd machine by $2$ times. At the same time, the farmer can leave is be as it is and the total power won't change.

In the third example, it is optimal to leave it be as it is.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` machines with powers `a_i`
2. We can perform at most one operation: choose two indices `i` and `j`, and an integer `x` such that `x` divides `a_i`
3. The operation changes: `a_i = a_i / x` and `a_j = a_j * x`
4. All powers must remain positive integers
5. We want to find the minimum possible total power after at most one such operation (or no operation)

## Key Insights

1. The total power before any operation is `sum(a_i)`
2. After the operation: new_total = `sum(a_i) - x + x = sum(a_i)` (the x is subtracted from a_i and added to a_j)

So the total power changes by: `a_i*(1/x - 1) + a_j*(x - 1)`

We want to minimize the total power, so we want this change to be as negative as possible.

## Strategy

1. We can either do nothing (total = original sum)
2. Or do one operation that decreases total power

When we change a_i from `a_i` to `a_i/x`, we lose `(a_i - a_i/x) = a_i*(1 - 1/x)`
When we change a_j from `a_j` to `a_j*x`, we gain `(a_j*x - a_j) = a_j*(x-1)`

So total change = `a_j*(x-1) - a_i*(1 - 1/x) = a_j*x - a_j - a_i + a_i/x`

This is not zero.

So to minimize total power, we want to minimize: `sum(a) + a_j*(x-1) - a_i*(1 - 1/x)`

Since the original sum is fixed, we just need to minimize the expression `a_j*(x-1) - a_i*(1 - 1/x)`.

For each pair (i,j) and each divisor x of a_i, we can compute this value and find the minimum.

But with n ≤ 50000, and each number having at most 12 divisors, the total operations would be O(n² * d) which is too slow.

We need a more efficient approach.

## Optimized Approach

Notice that for a fixed i and x, we want to minimize the expression `a_j*(x-1) - a_i*(1 - 1/x)`.

This expression is linear in a_j: `a_j*(x-1) - constant`

So for fixed i and x, the minimum occurs when we choose the smallest possible a_j (since x-1 could be positive or negative).

Let's analyze:
- If x > 1: x-1 > 0, so we want the smallest a_j
- If x = 1: x-1 = 0, so the expression becomes `-a_i*(1 - 1)` = 0
- If x < 1: not possible since x must be a positive divisor

So only x ≥ 1, and x divides a_i.

For x = 1: change = 0, so total remains same.

For x > 1: we want to minimize `a_j*(x-1) - a_i*(1 - 1/x)` 
Since x-1 > 0, we want the smallest possible a_j.

But wait: we want to minimize the total power, so we want the expression to be as negative as possible.

For x > 1: `a_j*(x-1)` is positive, and `-a_i*(1-1/x)` is negative.
So we want to minimize `a_j*(x-1)` which means choosing the smallest possible a_j.

Actually, if x > 1, then x-1 > 0, so larger a_j makes the first term larger, which makes the expression larger. Since we want to minimize the expression (make it as negative as possible), we want to minimize a_j.

Wait: if x > 1, then a_j*(x-1) is positive, and negative a_i*(1-1/x) is also negative. But since we can't make it negative by choosing small a_j, we need to see the net effect.

Actually, let's reconsider: we want to minimize the total power, so we want the expression `a_j*(x-1) - a_i*(1-1/x)` to be as negative as possible.

For x > 1:
- a_j*(x-1) ≥ 0
- -a_i*(1-1/x) < 0 (since 1-1/x > 0)

So the expression is negative + non-negative.

To minimize it (make it more negative), we want to make the negative term larger (more negative), which happens when a_i is large and x is large.

Wait, no: -a_i*(1-1/x) becomes more negative when a_i is larger or when x is larger.

So for x > 1:
- Larger x → 1-1/x → decreases → -a_i*(1-1/x) becomes more negative → better (smaller total)
- Larger a_i → -a_i*(1-1/x) becomes more negative → better (smaller total)

But a_i is fixed for a given i and x.

For fixed i and x, the value of the expression depends on a_j. Since we want to minimize the total power, and the expression is linear in a_j with coefficient (x-1), we have:
- If x > 1: coefficient (x-1) > 0, so we want the smallest possible a_j to minimize the expression (make it less positive)
- If x = 1: coefficient 0, expression is 0

Actually, since we want to minimize the expression (make it as negative as possible), and for x > 1 the expression is `positive_term + negative_term`, we want to minimize the positive term by choosing small a_j.

Yes, because if we have to add a positive amount for a_j, choosing a smaller a_j reduces that positive amount.

So for each machine i with value a_i, and each divisor x of a_i where x > 1:
- The best choice of j is the machine with minimum a_j (excluding i itself? or including?)
- Wait, does j have to be different from i? Yes, we choose two different machines.

So for each pair (i,j) where i ≠ j, and x divides a_i, x > 1, we can compute the change.

To optimize, for each i:
- Find all divisors x of a_i where x > 1
- For each such x, we want to find the minimum a_j among all j ≠ i
- Then compute the change for this x: `a_j*(x-1) - a_i*(1-1/x)`
- Track the minimum change across all possibilities

If no such operation gives a negative change, then we keep the original sum.

## Algorithm

1. Read input
2. Calculate original total sum
3. Find the minimum value among all elements (min_val) - this is the minimum a_j for any j
4. For each machine i:
   - Find all divisors x of a_i where x > 1
   - For each such x:
     - Calculate change = min_val * (x-1) - a_i * (1 - 1/x)
     - Update min_change if this change is smaller
5. If min_change < 0, then answer = original_sum + min_change
6. Otherwise, answer = original_sum

Wait, is min_val always available? What if we want to pick a_j that is not the global minimum? But we just argued that for x > 1, to minimize the expression, we should pick the smallest possible a_j.

Yes, because the expression is `a_j*(x-1) + (-a_i*(1-1/x))`, and for x > 1, x-1 > 0, so larger a_j increases the expression, making it less negative. So we want the smallest a_j.

But is there a case where picking a larger a_j might be better? Let's see: if x-1 is negative, but x > 1 means x-1 ≥ 0, so no.

Only x=1 is valid, which gives change 0.

So our approach should work.

However, we need to be careful: when we have multiple machines with the same value, taking the minimum is fine.

Also, we need to ensure that the resulting powers are positive integers, which they will be since x divides a_i, so a_i/x is integer, and a_j*x is integer, and positive since a_j ≥ 1, x ≥ 1.

I'll restructure the code:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total = sum(a)
min_val = min(a)

# Count frequency of each value
from collections import Counter
count = Counter(a)

# Find second minimum value
# If there are multiple min_val, second_min might be min_val, otherwise greater than min_val
sorted_vals = sorted(set(a))
if len(sorted_vals) == 1:
    second_min = min_val
else:
    second_min = sorted_vals[1]

# Now for each position i, we need to know the minimum value among j != i
# If the value at i is min_val and count[min_val] == 1, then min_excluding_i = second_min
# Otherwise, min_excluding_i = min_val

min_change = 0

for i in range(n):
    num = a[i]
    divisors = []
    x = 2
    while x * x <= num:
        if num % x == 0:
            divisors.append(x)
            if x != num // x:
                divisors.append(num // x)
        x += 1
    
    # For each divisor x > 1
    for x in divisors:
        # Find min value among all j != i
        if a[i] == min_val and count[min_val] == 1:
            min_excluding_i = second_min
        else:
            min_excluding_i = min_val
        
        # Calculate change: a_j*(x-1) - a_i*(1-1/x)
        # But a_j is min_excluding_i
        change = min_excluding_i * (x - 1) - a[i] * (1 - 1/x)
        
        if change < min_change:
            min_change = change

# If min_change is negative, we can achieve a smaller total
if min_change < 0:
    result = total + min_change
else:
    result = total

print(result)
```

One issue: what if there's no divisor x > 1? For example, prime numbers like 2,3,5,7, etc.

We handle this by the divisors loop - for a prime number, the only divisors >1 are itself, so we do x = num.

For example, a[i]=3: divisors >1: [3], so we do x=3.

And the calculation works.

Final code:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total = sum(a)
from collections import Counter
count = Counter(a)
sorted_vals = sorted(set(a))
min_val = sorted_vals[0]
if len(sorted_vals) == 1:
    second_min = min_val
else:
    second_min = sorted_vals[1]

min_change = 0

for i in range(n):
    num = a[i]
    # Find all divisors x of num where x > 1
    divisors = []
    x = 2
    while x * x <= num:
        if num % x == 0:
            divisors.append(x)
            if x != num // x:
                divisors.append(num // x)
        x += 1
    
    # For each divisor x > 1
    for x in divisors:
        # Find minimum value among j != i
        if a[i] == min_val and count[min_val] == 1:
            min_excluding_i = second_min
        else:
            min_excluding_i = min_val
        
        change = min_excluding_i * (x - 1) - a[i] * (1 - 1/x)
        if change < min_change:
            min_change = change

if min_change < 0:
    print(total + min_change)
else:
    print(total)
```

Wait, there's an issue: when num=1, it has no divisor >1, so we skip. That's correct because we can only divide by x>1 that divides 1.

Also, for any number, our divisor finding correctly includes all divisors >1.

But what about the expression `1 - 1/x`? We are using floating point. This might lead to precision issues.

Since all a_i ≤ 100, and x ≤ 100, we can avoid floating point.

Note: `a[i] * (1 - 1/x) = a[i] * (x - 1) / x`

So change = `min_excluding_i * (x - 1) - a[i] * (x - 1) / x`
= `(x - 1) * (min_excluding_i - a[i] / x)`

But we can compute it as `min_excluding_i * (x - 1) - a[i] * (x - 1) / x`

To avoid floating point, we can compute:
change = (min_excluding_i * (x - 1) * x - a[i] * (x - 1)) / x
= (x - 1) * (min_excluding_i * x - a[i]) / x

But we only care about sign for minimization. However, since we're comparing changes, and we're looking for the minimum, using floating point might have precision issues.

But the values of x, min_excluding_i, a[i] are integers ≤ 100, so the expression (x-1)*(min_excluding_i*x - a[i]) / x might be a non-integer.

Actually, the original change is real number, but we can compare the rational values.

Since we're only comparing `change < min_change`, and we want the minimum, we can compare fractions.

But the denominator is always positive (x>1), so we can compare the numerator of the fraction.

We have: change = A / x where A = (x-1) * (min_excluding_i * x - a[i])

So comparing changes is equivalent to comparing A values (since x>0).

Therefore, we can compute the numerator and compare integers.

So instead of computing floating point, we can compute:
numerator = (x - 1) * (min_excluding_i * x - a[i])

Then the change is positive when numerator > 0, negative when numerator < 0.

So we can compare the numerators (since denominator x > 0).

But we want the minimum change, so we want the smallest A.

Actually: for fixed x, we want to minimize A = (x-1)*(min_excluding_i*x - a[i])

Yes, since x>1, (x-1)>0, so minimizing A is equivalent to minimizing (min_excluding_i*x - a[i]).

But in our loop, we already have a fixed i and x, so we can compute the numerator.

Let me verify with example: a[i]=4, x=2, min_excluding_i=1
A = (2-1)*(1*2 - 4) = 1*(2-4) = -2
change = -2/2 = -1 ✓

So we can compute numerator = (x - 1) * (min_excluding_i * x - a[i])
Then compare numerators directly.

But note: we are looking for the minimum change, which corresponds to the minimum numerator (since denominator > 0).

So we can compare integers.

Let me update:


Wait, I made a mistake. The change is numerator / x, not numerator.

So when comparing changes, comparing numerators is valid because x > 0, so change1 < change2 iff numerator1 < numerator2.

But we're storing min_numerator as the minimum numerator, and we want to know if the total change is negative.

The total change is min_numerator / x, which is negative if min_numerator < 0.

But when we compute the final result, we need to add min_numerator / x to total.

However, min_numerator / x might not be integer.

But in our derivation, is the change always rational? Yes, but the final total might not be integer?

Wait, no: the original powers are integers, and we're transforming them: a_i becomes a_i/x, a_j becomes a_j*x, so both are integers because x divides a_i.

Therefore, the total power after transformation must be an integer.

But our change is computed as (a_j*x - a_j) + (a_i/x - a_i) = a_j*(x-1) + a_i*(1/x - 1)

For this to be integer: a_i/x must be integer (given that x divides a_i), so a_i/x is integer, and a_j*x is integer, so the sum is integer.

Therefore, the change is integer.

But our expression: change = a_j*(x-1) + a_i*(1/x - 1) = a_j*(x-1) - a_i*(1 - 1/x)

And 1 - 1/x = (x-1)/x, so -a_i*(x-1)/x

So total change = (x-1)*(a_j - a_i/x)

Since a_i/x is integer, let's write a_i/x = k, then total change = (x-1)*(a_j - k)

Now a_j and k are integers, so a_j - k is integer, and x-1 is integer, so change is integer.

In our numerator calculation: we have (x-1)*(min_excluding_i*x - a[i])
But min_excluding_i*x - a[i] = min_excluding_i*x - a[i]
And (x-1)*(min_excluding_i*x - a[i]) / x = (x-1)*(min_excluding_i*x - a[i]) / x

But we just showed that the actual change is (x-1)*(a_j - a_i/x)

Are these equal?

Let me compute:
(x-1)*(a_j - a_i/x) = (x-1)*a_j - (x-1)*a_i/x

Our expression: (x-1)*(min_excluding_i*x - a[i]) / x = (x-1)*min_excluding_i*x/x - (x-1)*a[i]/x = (x-1)*min_excluding_i - (x-1)*a[i]/x

These are not equal.

I made a mistake in the derivation.

Let me go back.

The total change is:
- Original pair: a_i + a_j
- New pair: a_i/x + a_j*x
- Change: (a_i/x + a_j*x) - (a_i + a_j) = a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j = a_i*(1/x - 1) + a_j*(x - 1)

So change = a_i*(1/x - 1) + a_j*(x - 1)

This is not the same as what I computed earlier.

I previously said change = a_j*(x-1) - a_i*(1 - 1/x), which is the same as a_j*(x-1) + a_i*(1/x - 1) ✓

Yes, it is the same.

So change = a_j*(x-1) + a_i*(1/x - 1)

And this equals: (x-1)*a_j + a_i*(1/x - 1)

To avoid floating point, we can write this as:
change = (x-1)*a_j + a_i*(1/x - 1)

But the term a_i*(1/x - 1) is not integer in general.

However, we know that x divides a_i, so let k = a_i / x, which is an integer.

Then a_i = k*x, so:
change = (x-1)*a_j + (k*x)*(1/x - 1) = (x-1)*a_j + k*(1 - 1) = (x-1)*a_j

Wait, that can't be right.

a_i*(1/x - 1) = (k*x)*(1/x - 1) = k*x*(1/x - 1) = k*(1 - x) = -k*(x-1)

So change = (x-1)*a_j - k*(x-1) = (x-1)*(a_j - k)

And k = a_i/x, which is an integer.

Therefore, change = (x-1)*(a_j - a_i/x)

This is an integer.

So the change is (x-1)*(a_j - k) where k = a_i/x.

And since a_j and k are integers, and x-1 is integer, the change is integer.

Therefore, we can compute the change as (x-1) * (a_j - a_i // x)

We don't need the floating point at all.

For each operation, we can compute:
k = a[i] // x  (since x divides a[i])
change = (x - 1) * (a_j - k)

And we want to minimize this change over all valid choices.

For fixed i and x, to minimize change, we want to minimize (x-1) * (a_j - k)

Since x-1 > 0 (x > 1), this is minimized when (a_j - k) is minimized.

So we want the smallest value of (a_j - k).

k is fixed for fixed i,x (k = a[i] // x), so we want to minimize a_j - k, which is minimized when a_j is minimized.

Therefore, we want the smallest a_j for j ≠ i.

And we already have that.

So for fixed i and x, the change is (x-1) * (min_excluding_i - a[i] // x)

And we compare this integer value.

Final algorithm:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

total = sum(a)
from collections import Counter
count = Counter(a)
sorted_vals = sorted(set(a))
min_val = sorted_vals[0]
if len(sorted_vals) == 1:
    second_min = min_val
else:
    second_min = sorted_vals[1]

min_change = 0  # will store the minimum change (which is an integer)

for i in range(n):
    num = a[i]
    # Find all divisors x of num where x > 1
    divisors = []
    x = 2
    while x * x <= num:
        if num % x == 0:
            divisors.append(x)
            if x != num // x:
                divisors.append(num // x)
        x += 1
    
    # For each divisor x > 1
    for x in divisors:
        # Find minimum value among j != i
        if a[i] == min_val and count[min_val] == 1:
            min_excluding_i = second_min
        else:
            min_excluding_i = min_val
        
        # k = a[i] // x (since x divides a[i])
        k = a[i] // x
        
        # Change = (x-1) * (min_excluding_i - k)
        change = (x - 1) * (min_excluding_i - k)
        
        if change < min_change:
            min_change = change

if min_change < 0:
    print(total + min_change)
else:
    print(total)
```

Let me verify with example 1: i=3, a[i]=4, x=2
- min_excluding_i = 1 (since 1 is min_val and appears once)
- k = 4 // 2 = 2
- change = (2-1) * (1 - 2) = 1 * (-1) = -1
- min_change = -1
- result = 15 + (-1) = 14 ✓

Example 2: i=1, a[i]=2, x=2
- min_excluding_i = 4 (since a[1]=2=min_val and count[2]=1)
- k = 2 // 2 = 1
- change = (2-1) * (4 - 1) = 1 * 3 = 3 > 0
- other operations give 0 or positive, so min_change = 0 → result = 14 ✓

Example 3: all changes non-negative, so result = 18 ✓

Perfect!

The code is now correct and avoids floating point precision issues.

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

One day Sasha visited the farmer 2D and his famous magnetic farm. On this farm, the crop grows due to the influence of a special magnetic field. Maintaining of the magnetic field is provided by $n$ machines, and the power of the $i$-th machine is $a_i$. 

This year 2D decided to cultivate a new culture, but what exactly he didn't say. For the successful growth of the new culture, it is necessary to slightly change the powers of the machines. 2D can at most once choose an arbitrary integer $x$, then choose one machine and reduce the power of its machine by $x$ times, and at the same time increase the power of one another machine by $x$ times (powers of all the machines must stay positive integers). Note that he may not do that if he wants. More formally, 2D can choose two such indices $i$ and $j$, and one integer $x$ such that $x$ is a divisor of $a_i$, and change powers as following: $a_i = \frac{a_i}{x}$, $a_j = a_j \cdot x$

Sasha is very curious, that's why he wants to calculate the minimum total power the farmer can reach. There are too many machines, and Sasha can't cope with computations, help him!


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer $n$ ($2 \le n \le 5 \cdot 10^4$) — the number of machines.

The second line contains $n$ integers $a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n$ ($1 \le a_i \le 100$) — the powers of the machines.


-----Output-----

Print one integer — minimum total power.


-----Examples-----
Input
5
1 2 3 4 5

Output
14

Input
4
4 2 4 4

Output
14

Input
5
2 4 2 3 7

Output
18



-----Note-----

In the first example, the farmer can reduce the power of the $4$-th machine by $2$ times, and increase the power of the $1$-st machine by $2$ times, then the powers will be: $[2, 2, 3, 2, 5]$.

In the second example, the farmer can reduce the power of the $3$-rd machine by $2$ times, and increase the power of the $2$-nd machine by $2$ times. At the same time, the farmer can leave is be as it is and the total power won't change.

In the third example, it is optimal to leave it be as it is.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` machines with powers `a_i`
2. We can perform at most one operation: choose two machines `i` and `j`, and an integer `x`, where `x` divides `a_i`
3. The operation: `a_i = a_i / x` and `a_j = a_j * x`
4. All powers must remain positive integers
5. We want to find the minimum possible total power after any number of such operations (0 or 1)

## Key Insights

1. The total sum of powers remains constant if we perform the operation, because `a_i/x + a_j*x = a_i + a_j` (since we're just transferring value from one machine to another).

2. However, when we divide `a_i` by `x` and multiply `a_j` by `x`, the new values are `a_i/x` and `a_j*x`. The sum is `a_i + a_j`, which equals the original sum.

3. This means that if we perform the operation, the total sum remains unchanged.

4. Therefore, the minimum total power we can achieve is either:
   - The original total sum (if we do nothing)
   - Or some other sum that equals the original total sum (since any valid operation preserves the sum)

Actually, looking at the total: we want minimum total power. So we can perform operations that decrease the total sum.

The goal is to minimize the total sum through valid operations.

So we need to find if there are operations that can decrease the total sum, and if so, by how much.

For a valid operation on machine i with power a_i and machine j with power a_j, choosing divisor x of a_i:
- New power of i: a_i / x
- New power of j: a_j * x
- Change in total sum: (a_j * x - a_j) + (a_i / x - a_i) = a_j(x-1) + a_i(1/x - 1)

We want to minimize the final total, which means we want to minimize this change (make it negative, as much as possible).

So the final total sum = original_sum + [a_j(x-1) + a_i(1/x - 1)]

We want to minimize this, so we want to minimize [a_j(x-1) + a_i(1/x - 1)].

Since x ≥ 1 and x divides a_i, and x = 1 is always valid, when x=1: change = a_j(0) + a_i(0) = 0.

For x > 1, the term a_j(x-1) is positive, and a_i(1/x - 1) is negative.

So change = a_j(x-1) - a_i(1 - 1/x) = a_j(x-1) - a_i((x-1)/x) = (x-1)(a_j - a_i/x)

So change = (x-1)(a_j - a_i/x)

We want to minimize the total sum, so we want this change to be as negative as possible.

So we want (x-1)(a_j - a_i/x) < 0.

This happens when (a_j - a_i/x) < 0, i.e., when a_j < a_i/x, or a_j*x < a_i.

So if we can find x > 1 that divides a_i such that a_j*x < a_i, then (x-1)(a_j - a_i/x) < 0, so the total sum decreases.

The decrease amount is (x-1)(a_j - a_i/x) = (x-1)(a_j*x - a_i)/x.

Since we want to minimize the sum, we want to maximize this negative value (make it more negative).

So for each pair (i,j) and divisor x of a_i where a_j*x < a_i, we can compute the potential decrease: (x-1)(a_j*x - a_i)/x.

But note: we can only perform one such operation.

So the problem reduces to: find the maximum possible decrease we can achieve, and add it to the original sum.

The final total power = original_sum + maximum_decrease.

But we need to check if such an operation is valid.

Since we want to maximize the negative change, we can try all valid pairs (i,j) and all divisors x of a_i (x > 1), and compute the change.

However, note that the powers are small (≤ 100), so we can iterate through all possibilities.

But n ≤ 50000, so we need to be efficient.

For each machine i, we can find all divisors of a_i that are > 1.

If so, the decrease is (x-1)*(a_j*x - a_i)/x.

We want to maximize this decrease (which will be negative, so we want the most negative value).

But since we want the minimum final sum, we want the maximum negative decrease.

So: min_final_sum = original_sum + max(decrease_over_all_valid_operations)

where decrease is (x-1)*(a_j*x - a_i)/x for valid (i,j,x).

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

original_sum = sum(a)

# Frequency array for values from 1 to 100
freq = [0] * 101  # index 0 unused, 1 to 100
for val in a:
    if val <= 100:
        freq[val] += 1

best_decrease = 0  # at least we can do nothing

for i in range(n):
    ai = a[i]
    # Find all divisors x of ai where x > 1
    divisors = []
    d = 2
    while d * d <= ai:
        if ai % d == 0:
            divisors.append(d)
            if d != ai // d:
                divisors.append(ai // d)
        d += 1
    
    # Also check if ai itself has a divisor >1 that is not found? 
    # We have d*d <= ai, so we get all divisors.
    # But note: if ai is prime, divisors will be only ai itself, but ai>1, so we have ai in divisors?
    # When d*d <= ai, if ai % d == 0, we add d and ai//d.
    # When d = ai, d*d = ai*ai > ai (if ai>1), so we won't get d=ai in the loop.
    # So we need to add ai if ai > 1? But ai>1, and if ai is prime, ai//d = ai/d = ai when d=1, so we don't get ai.
    # For example, ai=5: d from 2 to 2, 2*2=4<=5, 5%2!=0, so no divisor found.
    # So we need to add ai if ai > 1 and ai is prime?
    
    # Actually, if ai has divisor x>1, it must be a proper divisor or ai itself.
    # Since we only add proper divisors when d < ai//d, and ai//d might be ai when d=1.
    # But d starts from 2.
    
    # We need to add ai if ai > 1 and ai is greater than 1, and we haven't added it.
    # When d=1, we would add d=1 and ai//1=ai, but we start from d=2.
    
    # So if ai > 1 and ai is prime, then only divisors are 1 and ai, so we need to add ai.
    
    # But ai > 1, and we want x>1, so we add ai.
    
    # So if ai > 1, and the only divisors are 1 and ai, then we have to add ai.
    
    # How to check if ai is prime? We can check if we have any divisor in the range 2 to sqrt(ai).
    
    # Actually, if no divisor is found in 2 to sqrt(ai), then ai is prime.
    
    # So we can check: if after the loop, no divisor is found, then ai is prime, so add ai.
    
    # But we already have a list of divisors. We can simply check if ai > 1 and we haven't added it.
    # We can add ai at the end if ai > 1 and no divisor was found in 2 to sqrt(ai).
    
    # Actually, we didn't use the condition that divisor must be >1. We only added divisors >1 that are less than or equal to sqrt(ai).
    
    # We can recompute all divisors >1.
    
    # Let me restart: find all divisors x of ai with x>1.
    divisors = []
    for x in range(2, ai + 1):
        if ai % x == 0:
            divisors.append(x)
            # We don't need to break since we want all divisors
    # This is O(ai) per i, worst-case ai=100, so 99 per i, total O(n*100) = 5e6, acceptable.
    
    # But we can do better with sqrt, but for ai≤100, O(ai) is acceptable.
    
    # Actually, we can do the sqrt method and add ai if not already present.
    # But to keep it simple, use the direct loop.
    
    # Let me change: use the loop from 2 to ai.
    # But removing the previous while loop, and use:
    divisors = []
    for x in range(2, ai + 1):
        if ai % x == 0:
            divisors.append(x)
    
    # But this is O(ai) per i, and sum over i of ai is O(n * average(ai)) ≤ 5e4 * 50 = 2.5e6, acceptable.
    
    for x in divisors:
        threshold = ai / x
        max_val = -1
        # Check all v from 1 to int(threshold)-1
        for v in range(1, int(threshold)):
            if freq[v] > 0:
                if v == ai and freq[ai] == 1:
                    continue
                else:
                    if v > max_val:
                        max_val = v
        if max_val != -1:
            decrease = (x - 1) * (max_val * x - ai) // x
            if decrease < best_decrease:
                best_decrease = decrease

print(original_sum + best_decrease)
```

Also, in the inner loop, we have v from 1 to int(threshold)-1.

Since v is integer, v ≤ floor((ai-1)/x)

Actually, since ai and x are integers, ai//x is the floor of ai/x.

So v in range(1, ai//x)  (since v < ai/x, so v ≤ ai//x - 1? No)

For example, ai=4, x=2, ai/x=2, so v < 2, so v=1.

ai//x = 2, so range(1, 2) gives v=1 → correct.

Another: ai=5, x=2, ai/x=2.5, v < 2.5 → v=1,2
ai//x = 5//2 = 2 → range(1,2) gives v=1 → missing v=2.

Oh no! So we need v < ai/x, so v <= floor(ai/x - epsilon) = floor(ai/x) if ai/x is not integer? Actually, if ai/x is integer, v < ai/x → v <= ai/x - 1
If ai/x is not integer, v < ai/x → v <= floor(ai/x)

So v in range(1, ai//x + 1) if ai/x is not integer? 

Actually, for any real number r, the integers v satisfying v < r are v = 1, 2, ..., floor(r-1e-9)

floor(r) is the greatest integer ≤ r.

v < r → v ≤ floor(r) - 1 if r is integer, else v ≤ floor(r)

But when r is integer, v < r → v ≤ r-1
When r is not integer, v < r → v ≤ floor(r)

So we can write: v from 1 to floor(r - 1e-9) = 1 to r//1 if we use integer floor.

Actually, in Python, for positive integers ai and x, ai/x may not be integer.

But ai//x is the floor of ai/x.

So ai//x = floor(ai/x)

So v < ai/x → v ≤ floor(ai/x) - 1 if ai/x is integer, else v ≤ floor(ai/x)

But floor(ai/x) is ai//x.

So if ai % x == 0, then ai/x is integer, so v < ai/x → v ≤ ai//x - 1
If ai % x != 0, then v < ai/x → v ≤ ai//x

So we need to handle the case where ai is divisible by x.

Therefore, the range should be:

if ai % x == 0:
    max_v = ai // x - 1
else:
    max_v = ai // x

But we want v in range(1, max_v+1) if max_v >=1.

Actually, we want v from 1 to max_v (inclusive).

So we can do:

start = 1
end = ai // x
if ai % x == 0:
    end -= 1  # because v < ai/x, and ai/x is integer

So v in range(1, end+1)

Yes.

So in the inner loop:

    max_val = -1
    end = ai // x - 1
else:
    max_val = -1
    end = ai // x

if end < 1:
    continue  # no v in range

    if freq[v] > 0:
        if v == ai and freq[ai] == 1:

But we start max_val = -1, so initially.

Actually, we can do:

    end -= 1

    continue  # skip this x

This should be correct.

Let me test with first example: ai=4, x=2
ai%x=4%2=0 → end = 4//2 -1 = 2-1=1
v in range(1,2): v=1
freq[1]=1>0, v=1≠4, so valid → max_val=1
decrease = (2-1)*(1*2 - 4)//2 = 1*(-2)//2 = -1 ✓

Another: ai=6, x=2
ai%x=0 → end = 6//2 -1 = 3-1=2
v=1,2
freq: if values present, say 1 and 2, then max_val=2
decrease = (2-1)*(2*2 - 6)//2 = 1*(4-6)//2 = -2//2 = -1

Correct.

Now implement.

Also, we are iterating over all divisors x of ai with x>1, which we get from the loop from 2 to ai.

This should be efficient enough.

Let me write the final code.

Note: the range of v is from 1 to end (inclusive), which is at most 100, so inner loop is O(1).

Total complexity: O(n * ai * 1) = O(n * 100) = O(n), which is 5e4 * 100 = 5e6, acceptable.

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

original_sum = sum(a)

# Frequency array for values from 1 to 100
freq = [0] * 101  # index 0 unused, 1 to 100
for val in a:
    if val <= 100:
        freq[val] += 1

best_decrease = 0  # at least we can do nothing

for i in range(n):
    ai = a[i]
    # Find all divisors x of ai with x > 1
    divisors = []
    # Iterate from 2 to ai
    for x in range(2, ai + 1):
        if ai % x == 0:
            divisors.append(x)
    
    for x in divisors:
        # Calculate the upper bound for v
        # v must satisfy v < ai / x
        # v is positive integer
        end = ai // x
        if ai % x == 0:
            end -= 1  # because v < ai/x when ai/x is integer
        
        # If no valid v, skip
        if end < 1:
            continue
            
        max_val = -1
        # Check all v from 1 to end (inclusive)
        for v in range(1, end + 1):
            if freq[v] > 0:
                # Check if v is a[i] and only one occurrence
                if v == ai and freq[ai] == 1:
                    continue
                else:
                    if v > max_val:
                        max_val = v
        
        # If we found a valid max_val
        if max_val != -1:
            decrease = (x - 1) * (max_val * x - ai) // x
            if decrease < best_decrease:
                best_decrease = decrease

print(original_sum + best_decrease)
```

Let me test with the provided examples.

Example 1: [1,2,3,4,5] → should output 14
We have the expected operation at i=3 (a[i]=4), x=2, end=4//2 - 1 = 2-1=1, v=1, max_val=1, decrease = (1)*(1*2 - 4)//2 = -2//2 = -1
best_decrease = -1, sum = 15 + (-1) = 14 ✓

Example 2: [4,2,4,4] → sum=14, should output 14
No operation found → best_decrease=0 → sum=14 ✓

Example 3: [2,4,2,3,7] → sum=18, should output 18
No operation found → best_decrease=0 → sum=18 ✓

All match.

I think this is correct.

One more edge: what if ai=1? Then no divisor x>1, so no operation possible.

Yes, our loop from 2 to 1 is empty → no divisors → correct.

So final code:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

original_sum = sum(a)

# Frequency array for values from 1 to 100
freq = [0] * 101  # index 0 unused, 1 to 100
for val in a:
    if val <= 100:
        freq[val] += 1

best_decrease = 0  # at least we can do nothing

for i in range(n):
    ai = a[i]
    # Find all divisors x of ai with x > 1
    divisors = []
    # Iterate from 2 to ai
    for x in range(2, ai + 1):
        if ai % x == 0:
            divisors.append(x)
    
    for x in divisors:
        # Calculate the upper bound for v
        # v must satisfy v < ai / x
        end = ai // x
        if ai % x == 0:
            end -= 1  # because v < ai/x when ai/x is integer
        
        # If no valid v, skip
        if end < 1:
            continue
            
        max_val = -1
        # Check all v from 1 to end (inclusive)
        for v in range(1, end + 1):
            if freq[v] > 0:
                # Check if v is a[i] and only one occurrence
                if v == ai and freq[ai] == 1:
                    continue
                else:
                    if v > max_val:
                        max_val = v
        
        # If we found a valid max_val
        if max_val != -1:
            decrease = (x - 1) * (max_val * x - ai) // x
            if decrease < best_decrease:
                best_decrease = decrease

print(original_sum + best_decrease)
```

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code 
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

original_sum = sum(a)

# Frequency array for values from 1 to 100
freq = [0] * 101  # index 0 unused, 1 to 100
for val in a:
    if val <= 100:
        freq[val] += 1

best_decrease = 0  # at least we can do nothing

for i in range(n):
    ai = a[i]
    # Find all divisors x of ai with x > 1
    divisors = []
    # Iterate from 2 to ai
    for x in range(2, ai + 1):
        if ai % x == 0:
            divisors.append(x)
    
    for x in divisors:
        # Calculate the upper bound for v
        # v must satisfy v < ai / x
        end = ai // x
        if ai % x == 0:
            end -= 1  # because v < ai/x when ai/x is integer
        
        # If no valid v, skip
        if end < 1:
            continue
            
        max_val = -1
        # Check all v from 1 to end (inclusive)
        for v in range(1, end + 1):
            if freq[v] > 0:
                # Check if v is a[i] and only one occurrence
                if v == ai and freq[ai] == 1:
                    continue
                else:
                    if v > max_val:
                        max_val = v
        
        # If we found a valid max_val
        if max_val != -1:
            decrease = (x - 1) * (max_val * x - ai) // x
            if decrease < best_decrease:
                best_decrease = decrease

print(original_sum + best_decrease)

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

One day Sasha visited the farmer 2D and his famous magnetic farm. On this farm, the crop grows due to the influence of a special magnetic field. Maintaining of the magnetic field is provided by $n$ machines, and the power of the $i$-th machine is $a_i$. 

This year 2D decided to cultivate a new culture, but what exactly he didn't say. For the successful growth of the new culture, it is necessary to slightly change the powers of the machines. 2D can at most once choose an arbitrary integer $x$, then choose one machine and reduce the power of its machine by $x$ times, and at the same time increase the power of one another machine by $x$ times (powers of all the machines must stay positive integers). Note that he may not do that if he wants. More formally, 2D can choose two such indices $i$ and $j$, and one integer $x$ such that $x$ is a divisor of $a_i$, and change powers as following: $a_i = \frac{a_i}{x}$, $a_j = a_j \cdot x$

Sasha is very curious, that's why he wants to calculate the minimum total power the farmer can reach. There are too many machines, and Sasha can't cope with computations, help him!


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer $n$ ($2 \le n \le 5 \cdot 10^4$) — the number of machines.

The second line contains $n$ integers $a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n$ ($1 \le a_i \le 100$) — the powers of the machines.


-----Output-----

Print one integer — minimum total power.


-----Examples-----
Input
5
1 2 3 4 5

Output
14

Input
4
4 2 4 4

Output
14

Input
5
2 4 2 3 7

Output
18



-----Note-----

In the first example, the farmer can reduce the power of the $4$-th machine by $2$ times, and increase the power of the $1$-st machine by $2$ times, then the powers will be: $[2, 2, 3, 2, 5]$.

In the second example, the farmer can reduce the power of the $3$-rd machine by $2$ times, and increase the power of the $2$-nd machine by $2$ times. At the same time, the farmer can leave is be as it is and the total power won't change.

In the third example, it is optimal to leave it be as it is.
