I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` machines with powers `a_i`
2. We can perform at most one operation: choose two machines `i` and `j`, and an integer `x` such that:
   - `x` divides `a_i`
   - We change `a_i` to `a_i / x` and `a_j` to `a_j * x`
3. All powers must remain positive integers
4. We want to find the minimum possible total power after at most one such operation (or no operation)

## Key Insights

1. The total power before any operation is `sum(a_i)`
2. After the operation, the total power remains the same because `a_i / x + a_j * x = a_i + a_j` (since we're just transferring power from one machine to another)
3. Wait, let me verify this: if we reduce machine i by x and increase machine j by x, then the total power becomes:
   - `original_sum - a_i + a_i/x + a_j + (a_j * x) - a_j` = `original_sum - a_i + a_i/x + a_j * x - a_j`
   - This is not equal to original_sum.

Let me recalculate: We reduce machine i from `a_i` to `a_i/x`, so we lose `a_i - a_i/x = a_i(1 - 1/x)`.
We increase machine j from `a_j` to `a_j * x`, so we gain `a_j * x - a_j = a_j(x - 1)`.

So new total power = original_sum - a_i(1 - 1/x) + a_j(x - 1)

This means the total power can change!

Wait, I think I misunderstood. Let me re-read: "reduce the power of its machine by x times" - this means divide by x, not subtract x.

So if original power is `a_i`, after dividing by x, it becomes `a_i/x`.
If original power is `a_j`, after multiplying by x, it becomes `a_j * x`.

So the new total power is: `sum(a_i) - a_i + a_i/x + a_j * x - a_j = sum(a_i) + a_i/x - a_i + a_j * x - a_j`
= `sum(a_i) + a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)`

Since `a_i` and `a_j` are positive and `x >= 1`, we need to check when this expression is minimized.

But we're looking for the minimum total power. The key insight is that we want to minimize the total power.

For the total power to decrease, we need:
`a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1) < 0`

Let's analyze: `a_i(1/x - 1) = a_i(1 - x)/x = -a_i(x-1)/x`
So total change = `-a_i(x-1)/x + a_j(x-1) = (x-1)(a_j - a_i/x)`

For this to be negative, we need `(x-1)(a_j - a_i/x) < 0`

Case 1: `x = 1` → no change
Case 2: `x > 1` → we need `a_j - a_i/x < 0` → `a_j < a_i/x` → `a_j * x < a_i`

So if we can find `x > 1` such that `a_j * x < a_i`, then the total power decreases.

But we also need that `x` divides `a_i`.

So our strategy:
1. The baseline answer is the original sum
2. We can potentially decrease the total power by finding a valid operation
3. We need to find the minimum possible total power across all valid operations

Let me verify with the first example:
- Initial: [1,2,3,4,5], sum = 15
- After operation in example: [2,2,3,2,5], sum = 14
- So total power decreased from 15 to 14

For machine i=4 (power 4), machine j=0 (power 1):
- Can we divide 4 by x and multiply 1 by x?
- We need x to divide 4, so x ∈ {1,2,4}
- x=1: no change
- x=2: divide 4 by 2 → 2, multiply 1 by 2 → 2. New array: [2,2,3,2,5], sum = 14
- x=4: divide 4 by 4 → 1, multiply 1 by 4 → 4. New array: [4,2,3,1,5], sum = 15

So minimum is 14.

The algorithm:
1. Calculate original sum
2. For each pair (i,j) where i ≠ j:
   - For each divisor x of a_i (where x > 1, since x=1 gives no change):
     - If a_j * x < a_i (so that total power decreases):
       - Calculate new sum = original_sum - a_i + a_i/x + a_j * x
       - Update minimum sum
3. Return the minimum sum found

But this would be O(n² * sqrt(max(a_i))) which might be too slow for n=50000.

Wait, let me think about the constraints. Powers are at most 100, so divisors are limited.

But n can be up to 50,000, so O(n²) would be 2.5e9 operations which is too slow.

We need a more efficient approach.

Alternative approach:
- We want to minimize `original_sum + (x-1)(a_j - a_i/x)`
- For fixed i,j, we want to minimize this over divisors x of a_i where x > 1 and a_j * x < a_i

But we can't do O(n²) operations.

Let me reconsider: In most cases, especially with small values (each a_i ≤ 100), the reduction might be small. But we still need to be careful.

Wait, let's look at the constraints again: each a_i ≤ 100, and n ≤ 50,000.

Since each a_i is at most 100, we can precompute all divisors up to 100.

For each machine i, we can store all divisors of a_i.

But the pairing (i,j) is still O(n²).

But notice: the total power change is `a_i/x + a_j*x - a_i - a_j = (a_i/x - a_i) + (a_j*x - a_j) = a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)`

We want to minimize this. Since we're looking for minimum total power, we want to minimize this expression.

But the key insight might be that for most pairs, there's no improvement. Only when a_j is small and a_i is large relative to a_j can we get a decrease.

Since each a_i ≤ 100, and x ≥ 2, a_j * x ≥ 2 * 1 = 2, so we need a_i > 2 * a_j.

For a given machine i with power a_i, and a machine j with power a_j, we need a_i > 2 * a_j for any potential improvement.

So we only need to consider pairs where a_i > 2 * a_j.

Moreover, for each such pair, we try all divisors x of a_i where x > 1 and x ≤ a_i/a_j (since we need a_j * x < a_i, so x < a_i/a_j).

Since a_i ≤ 100 and a_j ≥ 1, a_i/a_j ≤ 100, but in our filter, a_i > 2*a_j, so a_j < a_i/2.

Given that a_i ≤ 100, the total number of such pairs might be reasonable.

Let's implement the O(n²) solution and hope it passes, since the actual number of pairs where a_i > 2*a_j might be small.

Actually, worst case: if we have many machines with powers around 50, then a_i > 2*a_j would require a_j < 25, so for each large a_i, we look at machines with small a_j.

With powers up to 100, and n=50000, the number of pairs where a_i > 2*a_j might still be large.

Alternative efficient approach:

Since we only need to check divisors of a_i (and there are at most 10 divisors for each a_i since a_i ≤ 100), we can precompute divisors.

For each possible divisor x (from 2 to 100), we can find which machines can be divided by x and which machines can receive the power.

But the operation is: divide machine i by x, multiply machine j by x.

So for each possible x (divisor) from 2 to 100:
- For each machine i where x divides a_i:
  - For each machine j where a_j * x < a_i:
    - Calculate new sum = original_sum - a_i + a_i/x + a_j * x
    - Update minimum sum

This is O(100 * n * n) which is still O(n²) and too slow.

Wait, for each x from 2 to 100:
- We need to iterate over machines i where x | a_i
- And for each such i, iterate over machines j where a_j * x < a_i

The total work would be sum over x of (number of i with x|a_i) * (number of j with a_j*x < a_i for each such i)

In worst case, if x=2, and half the machines have even values, then number of i is n/2, and for each such i, we check all j where a_j*2 < a_i.

Since a_j ≥ 1, a_j*2 ≥ 2, so a_i > 2, which is most values.

For each such i, we need to check all j. So O(n²) again.

But the frequencies: since a_i ≤ 100, a_j ≤ 100, and x ≤ 100, but the constraint a_j*x < a_i limits j.

For a fixed x, and fixed i with power a_i, we need a_j < a_i/x.

Since a_j ≠ 0, the number of such j is at most a_i/x.

So for each x, total work is sum over i where x|a_i of (a_i/x).

Since a_i ≤ 100, and x ≥ 2, a_i/x ≤ 50.

But sum over i of a_i/x = (1/x) * sum of a_i for all i where x|a_i.

The total over all x from 2 to 100 of (1/x) * sum of a_i for i where x|a_i.

Since each a_i ≤ 100, and the sum of a_i over all i is at most 50000*100 = 5e6, and we're dividing by x ≥ 2, this sum is at most (1/2) * 5e6 = 2.5e6, which is acceptable.

So total complexity is O(100 * (sum over i of (a_i / x for divisors x of a_i))) ≈ O(100 * sum of a_i / x over all divisors).

Since for each a_i, sum over divisors x of a_i of (a_i/x) = sum over divisors d of a_i of d (where d = a_i/x).

And sum over divisors d of a_i of d is the sum of divisors function.

For numbers up to 100, the sum of divisors is at most around 100*20/2 = 1000 (worst case: 96 has divisors 1,2,3,4,6,8,12,16,24,32,48,96 -> sum is large).

Actually maximum sum of divisors of a number ≤ 100 is for 96: 1+2+3+4+6+8+12+16+24+32+48+96 = 212.

So total sum over all a_i of (sum of divisors of a_i) is at most 50000 * 212 = 1.06e8, which is too large.

Wait, no: in our algorithm, for each x from 2 to 100, we're only considering divisors x of a_i, and for each such i, we're doing work proportional to a_i/x.

So total work = sum over x=2 to 100 of sum over i where x|a_i of (a_i/x)

This is equivalent to sum over all i, sum over divisors x of a_i where x ≥ 2 of (a_i/x)

For each i, this is sum over divisors x≥2 of a_i of (a_i/x) = sum over divisors d of a_i (where d = a_i/x) of d (since a_i/x = d, so x = a_i/d, and a_i/x = d)

This is the sum of all divisors of a_i that are ≥ 2.

For example, if a_i=6: divisors are 1,2,3,6. Sum of divisors ≥2: 2+3+6=11.

The maximum sum of divisors of a number ≤100 is for 96: sum of all divisors is 212, so sum of divisors ≥2 is 211.

So total work over all i is sum over i of (sum of divisors of a_i ≥ 2) ≤ 50000 * 211 = 1.055e7, which is acceptable in Python.

So algorithm:
1. Read input
2. Calculate original_sum
3. Initialize min_sum = original_sum
4. For each possible x from 2 to 100:
   - For each machine i (index i from 0 to n-1):
     - If x divides a_i:
       - For each machine j (index j from 0 to n-1):
         - If j != i and a_j * x < a_i:  # this ensures decrease
           - new_sum = original_sum - a_i + a_i//x + a_j * x
           - min_sum = min(min_sum, new_sum)
5. Print min_sum

But wait, in the operation, we can choose any two different machines i and j. In the above, i is the one being divided, j is the one being increased.

Also, in the condition a_j * x < a_i, this ensures the total power decreases (as shown in derivation).

But what if a_j * x = a_i? Then total power change = (x-1)(a_j - a_i/x) = (x-1)(a_j - a_j) = 0, so no change.

If a_j * x > a_i, then total power increases.

So only when a_j * x < a_i do we get a decrease.

We also need to ensure all powers remain positive integers, which they do since we're dividing a_i by x (and x divides a_i, so a_i/x is integer) and multiplying a_j by x (which remains integer).

So the algorithm is correct.

Let me test with the first example: [1,2,3,4,5], original_sum = 15

Try x=2:
- i=0 (a_i=1): 2 does not divide 1 → skip
- i=1 (a_i=2): 2 divides 2 → a_i//x = 1
  - j=0 (a_j=1): 1*2=2, which is not < 2 → skip (since 2 < 2 is false)
  - j=2 (a_j=3): 3*2=6 > 2 → skip
  - j=3 (a_j=4): 4*2=8 > 2 → skip
  - j=4 (a_j=5): 5*2=10 > 2 → skip
- i=2 (a_i=3): 2 does not divide 3 → skip
- i=3 (a_i=4): 2 divides 4 → a_i//x = 2
  - j=0 (a_j=1): 1*2=2 < 4 → valid
    - new_sum = 15 - 4 + 2 + 1*2 = 15 - 4 + 2 + 2 = 15
  - j=1 (a_j=2): 2*2=4 not < 4 → skip
  - j=2 (a_j=3): 3*2=6 > 4 → skip
  - j=4 (a_j=5): 5*2=10 > 4 → skip
- i=4 (a_i=5): 2 does not divide 5 → skip

Try x=3:
- i=0: 3 not divide 1 → skip
- i=1: 3 not divide 2 → skip
- i=2: 3 divides 3 → a_i//x = 1
  - j=0: 1*3=3 not < 3 → skip
  - j=1: 2*3=6 > 3 → skip
  - j=3: 4*3=12 > 3 → skip
  - j=4: 5*3=15 > 3 → skip
- i=3: 3 not divide 4 → skip
- i=4: 3 not divide 5 → skip

Try x=4:
- i=0: skip
- i=1: skip
- i=2: skip
- i=3: 4 divides 4 → a_i//x = 1
  - j=0: 1*4=4 not < 4 → skip
  - others: a_j*4 > 4 → skip
- i=4: skip

So the only valid operation found gives new_sum = 15, which is not less than 15.

But the example says output is 14.

Wait, in the example, they decreased machine 4 (power 4) by 2 times to get 2, and increased machine 1 (power 1) by 2 times to get 2. 

In my indexing: machine 4 is index 3 (0-indexed), machine 1 is index 0.

So for i=3, j=0, x=2: a_i=4, a_j=1, x=2
- x divides 4 → yes
- a_j * x = 1*2 = 2 < 4 → yes
- new_sum = 15 - 4 + 4//2 + 1*2 = 15 - 4 + 2 + 2 = 15

Wait, that gives 15, not 14.

What's wrong?

Original powers: [1,2,3,4,5], sum=15
After operation: machine i=3 (4th machine) becomes 4//2=2, machine j=0 (1st machine) becomes 1*2=2
New array: [2,2,3,2,5], sum = 2+2+3+2+5 = 14

But my calculation: 15 - 4 (original a_i=4) + 2 (new a_i=2) + 2 (new a_j=2) - 1 (original a_j=1) = 15 - 4 + 2 + 2 - 1 = 14

Ah! I made a mistake in the formula.

The total change is: 
- Remove original a_i and a_j
- Add new a_i = a_i/x and new a_j = a_j * x
- So net change = (a_i/x + a_j * x) - (a_i + a_j) = a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)

So new_sum = original_sum + a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)

In the example: 15 + 4*(1/2 - 1) + 1*(2 - 1) = 15 + 4*(-1/2) + 1*1 = 15 - 2 + 1 = 14

So my formula was wrong earlier. I said original_sum - a_i + a_i/x + a_j * x, but forgot to subtract a_j.

Correct formula: new_sum = original_sum - a_i - a_j + a_i/x + a_j * x

Or: new_sum = original_sum + (a_i/x - a_i) + (a_j * x - a_j) = original_sum + a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)

So I need to fix that.

In the loop, for each i,j,x:
- if x divides a_i and a_j * x < a_i:
- new_sum = original_sum - a_i - a_j + a_i//x + a_j * x
- min_sum = min(min_sum, new_sum)

Let me retest the first example with this:
x=2, i=3 (a_i=4), j=0 (a_j=1):
- new_sum = 15 - 4 - 1 + 4//2 + 1*2 = 15 - 5 + 2 + 2 = 14 ✓

Perfect.

Now the algorithm is:
1. Read n and array a
2. original_sum = sum(a)
3. min_sum = original_sum
4. For x from 2 to 100:
   - For each i in range(n):
     - If a[i] % x == 0:
       - For each j in range(n):
         - If j != i and a[j] * x < a[i]:
           - new_sum = original_sum - a[i] - a[j] + a[i]//x + a[j] * x
           - min_sum = min(min_sum, new_sum)
5. Print min_sum

But this is still O(100 * n * n) in worst case.

But as analyzed, the inner two loops are bounded by the sum over i of (a_i / x) for each x.

More precisely: for each x, we iterate over all i where x divides a_i, and for each such i, we iterate over all j where a[j]*x < a[i].

The number of such j for a given i is at most a[i]/x (since a[j] < a[i]/x), and since a[j] ≥ 1.

So total work for a fixed x is sum over i with x|a_i of (a[i]/x).

As shown, total over all x is sum over i of sum over divisors x≥2 of (a[i]/x) = sum over i of (sum of divisors of a[i] ≥ 2)

Since each a[i] ≤ 100, and the sum of divisors of a[i] ≥ 2 is at most ~210, and there are at most 50000 machines, total work is at most 50000 * 210 = 10.5e6, which is acceptable in Python.

So we can implement this.

Let me test with second example: [4,2,4,4], sum=14

Expected output: 14

Check if any operation can reduce sum.

Initially sum=14.

Try x=2:
- i=0 (a_i=4): divisible by 2
  - j=1 (a_j=2): 2*2=4 not < 4 → skip
  - j=2 (a_j=4): 4*2=8 > 4 → skip
  - j=3 (a_j=4): 4*2=8 > 4 → skip
- i=1 (a_i=2): 2 divides 2
  - j=0: 4*2=8 > 2 → skip
  - j=2: 4*2=8 > 2 → skip
  - j=3: 4*2=8 > 2 → skip
- i=2 (a_i=4): divisible by 2
  - j=1: 2*2=4 not < 4 → skip
  - j=0: 4*2=8 > 4 → skip
  - j=3: 4*2=8 > 4 → skip
- i=3 (a_i=4): divisible by 2
  - j=1: 2*2=4 not < 4 → skip
  - j=0: 8>4 → skip

Try x=4:
- i=0: divisible by 4 → a[0]=4
  - j=1: 2*4=8 > 4 → skip
  - j=2: 4*4=16 > 4 → skip
  - j=3: 4*4=16 > 4 → skip

  - j=2: 2*2=4 not < 4 → skip
  - j=3: 3*2=6 > 4 → skip
  - j=4: 7*2=14 > 4 → skip
- i=2 (a_i=2): divisible by 2
  - j=0: 2*2=4 > 2 → skip
  - j=1: 4*2=8 > 2 → skip
  - j=3: 3*2=6 > 2 → skip
  - j=4: 7*2=14 > 2 → skip
- i=3 (a_i=3): not divisible by 2
- i=4 (a_i=7): not divisible by 2

Try x=3:
- i=3 (a_i=3): divisible by 3
  - j=0: 2*3=6 > 3 → skip
  - j=1: 4*3=12 > 3 → skip
  - j=2: 2*3=6 > 3 → skip
  - j=4: 7*3=21 > 3 → skip

So no improvement. Answer is 18 ✓

But wait, the example says output is 18, which matches.

One more test: could we get a decrease with x=2, i=3, but a_i=3 is not divisible by 2.

No.

So the algorithm seems correct.

Let me code it.

Note: We must ensure j ≠ i. In the problem, it says "choose one machine and reduce... and increase one another machine", so different machines.

So in the loop, we require j ≠ i.

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

original_sum = sum(a)
min_sum = original_sum

# Try all possible x from 2 to 100 (since a_i <= 100)
for x in range(2, 101):
    for i in range(n):
        if a[i] % x == 0:
            # For current machine i, try to transfer to any other machine j
            ai = a[i]
            ai_div_x = ai // x
            # We need a[j] * x < ai (so that total power decreases)
            for j in range(n):
                if i == j:
                    continue
                aj = a[j]
                if aj * x < ai:
                    new_sum = original_sum - ai - aj + ai_div_x + aj * x
                    if new_sum < min_sum:
                        min_sum = new_sum

print(min_sum)
```

This should work. The complexity is acceptable as analyzed.

Let me run through first example with this code:
- a = [1,2,3,4,5], original_sum=15, min_sum=15
- x=2:
  - i=3 (a[3]=4): divisible by 2
    - j=0: aj=1, 1*2=2 < 4 → new_sum = 15 - 4 - 1 + 2 + 2 = 14 → min_sum=14
    - others: j=1: 2*2=4 not < 4 → skip; j=2: 3*2=6>4; j=4: 5*2=10>4
- All other x give no improvement
- Final answer: 14 ✓

Perfect.

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

One day Sasha visited the farmer 2D and his famous magnetic farm. On this farm, the crop grows due to the influence of a special magnetic field. Maintaining of the magnetic field is provided by $n$ machines, and the power of the $i$-th machine is $a_i$. 

This year 2D decided to cultivate a new culture, but what exactly he didn't say. For the successful growth of the new culture, it is necessary to slightly change the powers of the machines. 2D can at most once choose an arbitrary integer $x$, then choose one machine and reduce the power of its machine by $x$ times, and at the same time increase the power of one another machine by $x$ times (powers of all the machines must stay positive integers). Note that he may not do that if he wants. More formally, 2D can choose two such indices $i$ and $j$, and one integer $x$ such that $x$ is a divisor of $a_i$, and change powers as following: $a_i = \frac{a_i}{x}$, $a_j = a_j \cdot x$

Sasha is very curious, that's why he wants to calculate the minimum total power the farmer can reach. There are too many machines, and Sasha can't cope with computations, help him!


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer $n$ ($2 \le n \le 5 \cdot 10^4$) — the number of machines.

The second line contains $n$ integers $a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n$ ($1 \le a_i \le 100$) — the powers of the machines.


-----Output-----

Print one integer — minimum total power.


-----Examples-----
Input
5
1 2 3 4 5

Output
14

Input
4
4 2 4 4

Output
14

Input
5
2 4 2 3 7

Output
18



-----Note-----

In the first example, the farmer can reduce the power of the $4$-th machine by $2$ times, and increase the power of the $1$-st machine by $2$ times, then the powers will be: $[2, 2, 3, 2, 5]$.

In the second example, the farmer can reduce the power of the $3$-rd machine by $2$ times, and increase the power of the $2$-nd machine by $2$ times. At the same time, the farmer can leave is be as it is and the total power won't change.

In the third example, it is optimal to leave it be as it is.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` machines with powers `a_i`
2. We can perform at most one operation: choose two indices `i` and `j`, and an integer `x` such that:
   - `x` divides `a_i`
   - We transform: `a_i = a_i / x` and `a_j = a_j * x`
   - All powers must remain positive integers
3. We want to find the minimum possible total power after any number of such operations (0 or 1)

## Key Insights

1. The total sum of powers remains the same when we do the operation, because we're just transferring value from one machine to another.
2. When we do the operation on machines `i` and `j` with divisor `x`, the sum remains `a_i + a_j = (a_i/x) + (a_j*x)`.
3. Actually, let me verify: original sum = `a_i + a_j + sum of others`
   New sum = `(a_i/x) + (a_j*x) + sum of others` = `a_i + a_j + sum of others - a_i + a_i/x + a_j*x - a_j` = `original_sum + a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j`
   
Wait, this isn't right. Let me recalculate:
- Original contribution from machines i and j: `a_i + a_j`
- After operation: `a_i/x + a_j*x`
- Change in sum: `(a_i/x + a_j*x) - (a_i + a_j)` = `a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j` = `a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)`

This shows the sum can change. The sum only stays the same when `a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1) = 0`.

But let's think differently. The operation transfers value from machine i to machine j. When we divide `a_i` by `x` and multiply `a_j` by `x`, we're actually transferring `(a_i - a_i/x)` to `a_j`.

So the new sum is: `original_sum + (a_i - a_i/x) - (a_i - a_i/x)` = original_sum.

Wait, that can't be right either. Let me trace through the example:

Example 1: `[1,2,3,4,5]`, choose machine 4 (value 4) and machine 1 (value 1), with x=2.
- Machine 4 becomes 4/2 = 2
- Machine 1 becomes 1*2 = 2
- New array: `[2,2,3,2,5]`
- Original sum: 1+2+3+4+5 = 15
- New sum: 2+2+3+2+5 = 14

Ah! The sum decreased from 15 to 14. So the operation changes the total sum.

The change is: `(a_i/x) + a_j*x - (a_i + a_j) = a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j = a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)`

Since `x >= 1`, we have `1/x - 1 <= 0`, and `x - 1 >= 0`. 

For the sum to decrease, we need `a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1) < 0`.

But actually, we can make the sum smaller by choosing appropriate x, i, j.

The key insight is that we want to minimize the total power, so we want to make it as small as possible through this operation.

Since we can only do this operation once, and we want the minimum total power, we need to find if there's any operation that reduces the sum.

Let me think about what happens in the operation:
- We go from `a_i` to `a_i/x`
- We go from `a_j` to `a_j*x`
- The change in total sum is: `(a_i/x - a_i) + (a_j*x - a_j)` = `a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)`

We want this to be negative to minimize the total power.

So we need: `a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1) < 0`

This can be rewritten as: `a_i(1 - x)/x + a_j(x - 1) < 0`

Or: `(x - 1)(a_j - a_i/x) < 0`

Since `x - 1 >= 0` (x is a positive integer divisor), when `x > 1`, we need `(a_j - a_i/x) < 0`, which means `a_j < a_i/x`.

When `x = 1`, the change is `a_i(1/1 - 1) + a_j(1 - 1) = 0`, so sum doesn't change.

So for `x > 1`, we need `a_j < a_i/x`, which means `a_i/x > a_j`, so `a_i > a_j*x`.

Therefore, to get a reduction in the total sum, we need to find indices i, j and x>1 such that `a_i > a_j * x` and x divides `a_i`.

The reduction amount is: `a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1) = a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j`

So the new total power is: `original_sum + (a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j)`

We want to minimize this, so we want to maximize the negative contribution.

Wait, actually we want the minimum total power, so we want to minimize the final sum.

The final sum = original_sum + (a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j)

We need to find the minimum over all valid choices of i, j, x>1.

But the constraints are small: a_i ≤ 100, n ≤ 50000.

Since a_i ≤ 100, the divisors of any a_i are limited (at most 100 divisors for any number).

So we can try all possible pairs of machines and all divisors x of a_i for each machine i.

For each pair (i, j) and each divisor x of a_i with x > 1:
- Check if a_j < a_i / x (which is equivalent to a_i > a_j * x)
- If yes, then the new sum is original_sum + (a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j)
- Track the minimum such sum

Additionally, we always have the option to do nothing, so the answer is at least the original sum.

So algorithm:
1. Read input
2. Compute original_sum
3. Initialize min_sum = original_sum
4. For each machine i (index from 0 to n-1):
   - For each divisor x of a_i where x > 1:
     - For each machine j (index from 0 to n-1):
       - If a_i > a_j * x (so that a_j < a_i/x):
         - Compute new_sum = original_sum + (a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j)
         - Update min_sum = min(min_sum, new_sum)
5. Print min_sum

Wait, let me verify with the examples.

Example 1: [1,2,3,4,5], original_sum = 15
We found a solution with sum 14, so answer should be 14.

Example 2: [4,2,4,4], original_sum = 14
Answer is 14, so we should not be able to reduce it below 14.

For i=0, a_i=4, divisors >1: x=2,4
- x=2: need a_j < 4/2 = 2, so a_j < 2. Machines with a_j < 2: only machine j=1 (value 2 is not < 2), so no valid j
- x=4: need a_j < 4/4 = 1, so a_j < 1. No machine has a_j < 1.

For i=1, a_i=2, divisors >1: x=2
- x=2: need a_j < 2/2 = 1. No machine has a_j < 1.

For i=2, a_i=4, same as i=0
For i=3, a_i=4, same as i=0

So no reduction possible, min_sum = 14. ✓

Example 3: [2,4,2,3,7], original_sum = 18
Answer is 18, so should not be reducible.

Check if any operation reduces sum:
We need to find i,j,x>1 such that a_i > a_j * x and a_i divisible by x.

For i with a_i=2: divisors >1: none (2 has only divisors 1,2, so x=2)
For i with a_i=4: divisors >1: 2,4
- x=2: need a_j < 4/2 = 2, so a_j < 2. Machines with values <2: none (values 2,4,2,3,7)
- x=4: need a_j < 4/4 = 1. None
For i with a_i=3: divisors >1: 3
- x=3: need a_j < 3/3 = 1. None
For i with a_i=7: divisors >1: 7
- x=7: need a_j < 7/7 = 1. None

So indeed, no reduction possible, answer is 18. ✓

The algorithm looks correct.

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

original_sum = sum(a)
min_sum = original_sum

# Get all divisors for each number
def get_divisors(num):
    divisors = []
    for x in range(1, int(num**0.5) + 1):
        if num % x == 0:
            divisors.append(x)
            if x != num // x:
                divisors.append(num // x)
    return divisors

# For each machine i
for i in range(n):
    divisors = get_divisors(a[i])
    # Consider all divisors x > 1
    for x in divisors:
        if x > 1:
            # For each machine j
            for j in range(n):
                # We need a_j < a_i / x for the operation to reduce sum
                if a[j] < a[i] / x:
                    # Calculate the change in sum
                    change = (a[i] / x - a[i]) + (a[j] * x - a[j])
                    new_sum = original_sum + change
                    if new_sum < min_sum:
                        min_sum = new_sum

print(int(min_sum))
```

n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

original_sum = sum(a)
min_sum = original_sum

# Get all divisors for each number
def get_divisors(num):
    divisors = []
    for x in range(1, int(num**0.5) + 1):
        if num % x == 0:
            divisors.append(x)
            if x != num // x:
                divisors.append(num // x)
    return divisors

# For each machine i
for i in range(n):
    divisors = get_divisors(a[i])
    # Consider all divisors x > 1
    for x in divisors:
        if x > 1:
            # For each machine j
            for j in range(n):
                # We need a_j < a_i / x for the operation to reduce sum
                if a[j] < a[i] / x:
                    # Calculate the change in sum
                    change = (a[i] / x - a[i]) + (a[j] * x - a[j])
                    new_sum = original_sum + change
                    if new_sum < min_sum:
                        min_sum = new_sum

print(int(min_sum))

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` machines with powers `a_i`
2. We can perform at most one operation: choose two machines `i` and `j`, and an integer `x` such that:
   - `x` divides `a_i`
   - `a_i` becomes `a_i / x`
   - `a_j` becomes `a_j * x`
   - All powers must remain positive integers
3. We want to find the minimum possible total power after at most one such operation

## Key Insights

1. The total power of the system remains unchanged if we don't perform any operation (since `a_i/x + a_j*x = a_i + a_j` when we just transfer value)

2. When we perform the operation, the total power remains the same - this is because we're just transferring value from one machine to another. The sum `a_i/x + a_j*x` equals `a_i + a_j` only if `x` divides `a_i`.

Wait, let me verify this:
- Before: `a_i + a_j`
- After: `a_i/x + a_j*x`
- Is `a_i/x + a_j*x = a_i + a_j`? Only if `a_i/x = a_i` and `a_j*x = a_j`, which is not generally true.

Actually, let me recalculate:
- Original sum: `sum(a_k)` for all k
- After operation: `(a_i/x) + (a_j*x) + sum of other machines`
- This equals: `a_i + a_j + x*(a_j - a_i) + sum of other machines - a_i - a_j`
- So the total changes by `x*(a_j - a_i)`

Wait, that can't be right. Let me think again:
- Original: `a_i + a_j` for these two
- New: `a_i/x + a_j*x`
- Change: `(a_i/x + a_j*x) - (a_i + a_j) = a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j = a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)`

So the total power changes by `a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)`

For this to be negative (to minimize total power), we need `a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1) < 0`.

Let me simplify: `a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1) = a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j = (a_i/x + a_j*x) - (a_i + a_j)`

The total power after operation is: `original_total - a_i - a_j + (a_i/x + a_j*x)`

So the change is: `a_i/x + a_j*x - a_i - a_j`

To minimize the total power, we want to minimize `a_i/x + a_j*x`.

This means we want to find indices `i, j` and divisor `x` of `a_i` such that `a_i/x + a_j*x` is minimized.

But we're looking for the global minimum total power.

## Key Insight

Actually, let's reconsider: the total power after operation is simply `a_i/x + a_j*x + sum of all other a_k`.

This equals `original_total + (a_i/x + a_j*x - a_i - a_j)`.

So we want to minimize `(a_i/x + a_j*x - a_i - a_j)`.

Let me define `f(x) = a_i/x + a_j*x - a_i - a_j = (a_i/x - a_i) + (a_j*x - a_j) = a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)`.

For fixed `i, j`, we want to minimize `f(x)` over all divisors `x` of `a_i`.

Since `x ≥ 1`, and `x` divides `a_i`, the divisors are limited.

## Strategy

1. The minimum possible total power is at least the original total (when no operation is performed)
2. We might be able to achieve a lower total by transferring power
3. We need to check all possible pairs `(i, j)` and all divisors `x` of `a_i` to see if we can get a lower total

But let's think about when the total can decrease:
- We want `a_i/x + a_j*x < a_i + a_j`
- This means `a_i/x + a_j*x - a_i - a_j < 0`
- `a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1) < 0`
- `a_i(1/x - 1) < a_j(1 - x)`

For this to be possible, we need `x > 1` (since if `x=1`, then the operation doesn't change anything).

When `x > 1`, `1/x - 1 < 0` and `x - 1 > 0`, so `a_i(1/x - 1)` is negative and `a_j(x - 1)` is positive.

For the sum to be negative, we need `a_i(1/x - 1) < -a_j(x - 1)`.

Since `1/x - 1 = (1 - x)/x`, we have:
`a_i(1 - x)/x < -a_j(x - 1)`
`a_i(1 - x)/x < a_j(1 - x)` 
`(1 - x)(a_i/x - a_j) < 0`

Since `1 - x < 0` when `x > 1`, we need:
`a_i/x - a_j > 0` (so that the product is negative)
This means `a_i/x > a_j`

So we need `a_i/x > a_j`, which implies `a_i > a_j*x`.

Given that `x` divides `a_i`, we can iterate through all divisors.

## Algorithm

1. Calculate the original total sum
2. For each pair of machines `(i, j)` where `i ≠ j`:
   - For each divisor `x` of `a_i` where `x > 1`:
     - Check if `a_i/x > a_j` (this is equivalent to our condition for potential decrease)
     - Calculate the new total: `original_total + (a_i/x + a_j*x - a_i - a_j)`
     - Keep track of the minimum total found
3. The answer is the minimum total including the original total (when no operation is performed)

Wait, but checking all pairs might be too slow. Let's analyze constraints:
- `n ≤ 50000`, but each `a_i ≤ 100`
- The number of divisors for each `a_i` is at most around 12 (since 100 has 9 divisors)

But with `n` up to 50000, checking all pairs would be O(n² × d) which is about 50000² × 12 ≈ 3×10¹¹, which is too slow.

We need a more efficient approach.

## Optimized Insight

Instead of checking all pairs, notice that:
- We only care about whether we can get a total less than the original
- We want to minimize `a_i/x + a_j*x - a_i - a_j`

For a fixed `i`, we want to find the minimum value of `a_i/x + a_j*x - a_i - a_j` over all divisors `x` of `a_i` and all `j ≠ i`.

For fixed `i`, we can precompute for each divisor `x` of `a_i`, the minimum value of `(a_i/x + a_j*x - a_i - a_j)` over all `j ≠ i`.

This is equivalent to: for each divisor `x`, we want to minimize `a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j = a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)`

Since `x` is fixed, this becomes: `a_i(1/x - 1) + (x - 1) * a_j`

For fixed `x`, this is a linear function in `a_j`. To minimize it, we want the smallest possible `a_j` (since `x > 1`, `x - 1 > 0`).

So for fixed `i` and `x`, the minimum occurs when `a_j` is the minimum among all other values.

But we also need to ensure that `a_i/x > a_j` for the decrease to be possible.

Actually, let me reconsider the condition for decrease: we want `a_i/x + a_j*x < a_i + a_j`, which simplifies to `a_i/x > a_j + a_i - a_j*x`.

Wait, I think I made an error in the algebra.

Let's go back: we want `a_i/x + a_j*x < a_i + a_j`
This means `a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j < 0`
`a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1) < 0`

For a fixed `i` and `x`, we want to minimize the expression `a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)`.

Since `x > 1`, `x - 1 > 0`, so to minimize this expression, we want the smallest possible `a_j`.

So for each machine `i`, and each divisor `x > 1` of `a_i`, we:
1. Find the minimum value `min_val` among all other machines
2. Calculate: `a_i(1/x - 1) + min_val*(x - 1)`
3. If this is negative, then we can achieve a lower total

The decrease amount is `a_i(1/x - 1) + min_val*(x - 1)`

So the new total is `original_total + decrease`.

We need to find the minimum over all such decreases.

But we also need to ensure that during the operation, all values stay positive, which they will since we're only requiring `x` divides `a_i` and `a_i/x` is integer.

## Implementation Steps

1. Read input
2. Calculate original total sum
3. Find global minimum value (for use in calculations)
4. For each machine `i`:
   - For each divisor `x > 1` of `a_i`:
     - Find the minimum value among all other machines (except machine `i`)
     - Calculate the change: `a_i(1/x - 1) + min_other * (x - 1)`
     - If this change is negative, update the minimum total to `original_total + change`
5. The answer is the minimum total found (which could be the original total if no negative change)

Wait, but what if we can get a negative change? We need to check all possibilities.

Actually, since we want the minimum total, and the original total is one possibility, we take min(original_total, original_total + all_negative_changes).

But I think the original total might be the minimum in many cases.

Let me verify with the examples:

- Can we get lower than 15?

- Change: 4*(1/2 - 1) + 2*(2-1) = 4*(-0.5) + 2*1 = -2 + 2 = 0
- For x=4: 4/4 = 1, change: 4*(1/4 - 1) + 2*(4-1) = 4*(-0.75) + 2*3 = -3 + 6 = 3 > 0

- For other values (2,3,7), their divisors >1:

## Optimization

We need:
- The global minimum value among all machines (for when we pick the min other machine)
- But actually, when we're considering machine `i`, we need the minimum among all other machines.

We can precompute the global minimum, but be careful: when we're considering machine `i`, we exclude it.

So we can precompute the minimum value among all machines, and for each machine, if the global minimum is not in that machine, we can use it.

Actually, we need to handle cases where the minimum is exactly equal to the current machine's value.

So for each machine `i`, we need the minimum among all other machines.

We can do this by:
- First, find the global minimum value
- Then for each machine `i`, if `a_i` is not the global minimum, then min_other = global_min
- If `a_i` is the global minimum, then we need to find the second smallest value among all machines.

Actually, we need the second smallest value when the global minimum appears only once, but if multiple machines have the global minimum, then when we exclude one machine, the min_other is still the global minimum.

So if there are multiple machines with global_min, then when we exclude one, min_other = global_min
If there's only one machine with global_min, then when we exclude that machine, min_other = second_smallest

So algorithm:
1. Precompute global_min and count
2. Find second_smallest
3. For each machine `i`:
   - If `a_i` == global_min and count == 1: then min_other = second_smallest
   - Else: min_other = global_min
   - For each divisor `x > 1` of `a_i`:
     - Compute change = `a_i*(1/x - 1) + min_other*(x - 1)`
     - If change < 0, update min_total = original_total + change

But wait: in our example with [1,2,3,4,5], when i=3 (value 4), x=2:
- `a_i = 4`, `x = 2`
- change = `4*(1/2 - 1) + min_other*(2 - 1) = 4*(-0.5) + min_other*1 = -2 + min_other`
- min_other when excluding machine with value 4: min of [1,2,3,5] = 1
- change = -2 + 1 = -1 < 0
- So new total = 15 + (-1) = 14

Yes, this works.

But in the formula, I have `a_i/x + a_j*x - a_i - a_j`, which is `a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)`.

In our calculation, we use `min_other` as the value of `a_j`.

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

original_total = sum(a)

# Find global minimum and second minimum
sorted_vals = sorted(a)
global_min = sorted_vals[0]
second_min = sorted_vals[1] if n > 1 else float('inf')

# Count occurrences of global_min
count_min = a.count(global_min)

# For each machine i, find min_other
min_other = [float('inf')] * n
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] == global_min:
        if count_min == 1:
            # Only one machine has global_min, exclude this one
            # Find second smallest
            remaining = a[:]
            remaining.pop(i)
            min_other[i] = min(remaining)
        else:
            # Multiple machines have global_min, exclude this one, min_other is still global_min
            min_other[i] = global_min
    else:
        # This machine doesn't have global_min, exclude it, min_other is global_min
        min_other[i] = global_min

# Find divisors of each a[i] that are > 1
def get_divisors(num):
    divisors = []
    for x in range(2, num + 1):
        if num % x == 0:
            divisors.append(x)
    return divisors

min_total = original_total  # at least the original total

# For each machine i
for i in range(n):
    divisors = get_divisors(a[i])
    for x in divisors:
        change = a[i] * (1/x - 1) + min_other[i] * (x - 1)
        if change < 0:
            min_total = min(min_total, original_total + change)

print(min_total)
```

Wait, I think there's a simpler way. The condition `a_i/x > a_j` might not be necessary - our derivation shows we just need the change to be negative.

But let me verify if `a_i/x > a_j` is equivalent to our negative change condition.

We have: change = `a_i(1/x - 1) + a_j(x - 1)` = `a_i/x - a_i + a_j*x - a_j`

For this to be negative: `a_i/x + a_j*x < a_i + a_j`

This is the condition we want.

In our implementation, for each possible transfer, we compute the exact change.

But let me check the performance: each number ≤ 100 has at most 9 divisors (since 100 has divisors 1,2,4,5,10,20,25,50,100), so we have at most 9 divisors for each number.

With n ≤ 50000, total operations is at most 50000 * 9 = 450000, which is acceptable.

I think this should work.

Example 3: [2,4,2,3,7], sum=18
- For values:

    - change = 2*(1/2-1) + 2*(2-1) = 2*(-0.5) + 2*1 = -1 + 2 = 1 > 0
    - If exclude the other 2: min of [2,4,3,7] = 2

Perfect!

One more thing: we need to compute the minimum among other values correctly.

In the code, for each machine i, when we compute min_other[i], we need to find the minimum among all values except a[i].

We can do this by:
- For each i, we can iterate through all other j and take min(a[j] for j != i)
- But this would be O(n²), which with n=50000 is 2.5e9, too slow.

We need to precompute it efficiently.

We can do:
- Sort the array and find the minimum
- For each index i, if the global minimum appears only once and at position i, then min_other[i] = second_min
- Otherwise, min_other[i] = global_min

Actually, if there are duplicates of global_min, then even when we exclude one occurrence, the minimum of the rest is still global_min.

So:
- Precompute global_min and second_min
- Count how many times global_min appears
- For each i:
  - if a[i] == global_min and count_min == 1, then min_other[i] = second_min (since we exclude the only occurrence)
  - else: min_other[i] = global_min

This is correct.

Let me implement it.

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

original_total = sum(a)

# Find global min and second min
sorted_vals = sorted(a)
global_min = sorted_vals[0]
second_min = sorted_vals[1] if n > 1 else float('inf')

# Count occurrences of global_min
count_min = a.count(global_min)

# For each machine i, find min_other (minimum among all other machines)
min_other = [0] * n
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] == global_min:
        if count_min == 1:
            # Only one machine has global_min, so when we exclude it, min_other is second_min
            min_other[i] = second_min
        else:
            # Multiple machines have global_min, so when we exclude one, minimum is still global_min
            min_other[i] = global_min
    else:
        # This machine doesn't have global_min, so when we exclude it, minimum is still global_min
        min_other[i] = global_min

# Find divisors of each number > 1
def get_divisors(num):
    divisors = []
    for x in range(2, num + 1):
        if num % x == 0:
            divisors.append(x)
    return divisors

min_total = original_total  # at least the original total

# For each machine i
for i in range(n):
    divisors = get_divisors(a[i])
    for x in divisors:
        # Calculate the change in total power
        change = a[i] * (1/x - 1) + min_other[i] * (x - 1)
        if change < 0:
            min_total = min(min_total, original_total + change)

print(min_total)
```

This should be the solution.

Wait, what if there are multiple occurrences of global_min? For example, [1,1,2,3], n=4:
- global_min = 1, count_min = 2, second_min = 1
- For each machine with value 1:
  - a[i] == global_min, count_min = 2 > 1, so min_other[i] = global_min = 1
- For machine with value 2:
  - a[i] != global_min, so min_other[i] = 1
- For a machine with value 3:
  - min_other[i] = 1

This seems correct because even if we exclude one machine with value 1, there's still another machine with value 1.

So the minimum among other values is still 1.

Yes.

Final code:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

original_total = sum(a)

# Find global min and second min
sorted_vals = sorted(a)
global_min = sorted_vals[0]
second_min = sorted_vals[1] if n > 1 else float('inf')

# Count occurrences of global_min
count_min = a.count(global_min)

# For each machine i, find min_other (minimum among all other machines)
min_other = [0] * n
for i in range(n):
    if a[i] == global_min:
        if count_min == 1:
            # Only one machine has global_min, so when we exclude it, min_other is second_min
            min_other[i] = second_min
        else:
            # Multiple machines have global_min, so minimum among others is global_min
            min_other[i] = global_min
    else:
        # This machine doesn't have global_min, so minimum among others is global_min
        min_other[i] = global_min

# Find divisors of each number > 1
def get_divisors(num):
    divisors = []
    for x in range(2, num + 1):
        if num % x == 0:
            divisors.append(x)
    return divisors

min_total = original_total  # at least the original total

# For each machine i
for i in range(n):
    divisors = get_divisors(a[i])
    for x in divisors:
        # Calculate the change in total power
        change = a[i] * (1/x - 1) + min_other[i] * (x - 1)
        if change < 0:
            min_total = min(min_total, original_total + change)

print(min_total)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

One day Sasha visited the farmer 2D and his famous magnetic farm. On this farm, the crop grows due to the influence of a special magnetic field. Maintaining of the magnetic field is provided by $n$ machines, and the power of the $i$-th machine is $a_i$. 

This year 2D decided to cultivate a new culture, but what exactly he didn't say. For the successful growth of the new culture, it is necessary to slightly change the powers of the machines. 2D can at most once choose an arbitrary integer $x$, then choose one machine and reduce the power of its machine by $x$ times, and at the same time increase the power of one another machine by $x$ times (powers of all the machines must stay positive integers). Note that he may not do that if he wants. More formally, 2D can choose two such indices $i$ and $j$, and one integer $x$ such that $x$ is a divisor of $a_i$, and change powers as following: $a_i = \frac{a_i}{x}$, $a_j = a_j \cdot x$

Sasha is very curious, that's why he wants to calculate the minimum total power the farmer can reach. There are too many machines, and Sasha can't cope with computations, help him!


-----Input-----

The first line contains one integer $n$ ($2 \le n \le 5 \cdot 10^4$) — the number of machines.

The second line contains $n$ integers $a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_n$ ($1 \le a_i \le 100$) — the powers of the machines.


-----Output-----

Print one integer — minimum total power.


-----Examples-----
Input
5
1 2 3 4 5

Output
14

Input
4
4 2 4 4

Output
14

Input
5
2 4 2 3 7

Output
18



-----Note-----

In the first example, the farmer can reduce the power of the $4$-th machine by $2$ times, and increase the power of the $1$-st machine by $2$ times, then the powers will be: $[2, 2, 3, 2, 5]$.

In the second example, the farmer can reduce the power of the $3$-rd machine by $2$ times, and increase the power of the $2$-nd machine by $2$ times. At the same time, the farmer can leave is be as it is and the total power won't change.

In the third example, it is optimal to leave it be as it is.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

And for each such (i,x), we only need the minimum a_j for j≠i.

So the algorithm:

1. Read input
2. Compute original_sum
3. Precompute the minimum value for each position excluding itself (min_excl[i])
4. For each machine i:
   - Find all divisors x>1 of a_i
   - For each such x:
     - If x < 1 + a_i/2:  # this ensures change might be negative
       - Find min_other = min_excl[i]
       - Compute change = min_other*(x-1) + a_i*(1/x - 1)
       - If change < 0:
           new_sum = original_sum + change
           min_total = min(min_total, new_sum)
5. Answer is min_total (or original_sum if no reduction)

But we might miss cases where a_j is not the global minimum but gives negative change? No, because for fixed i,x, since the change is linear in a_j with positive coefficient (x-1>0), smaller a_j gives smaller change.

So yes, for fixed i,x, the minimal change is achieved when a_j is minimal.

So we can do this.

But is it safe to only consider x < 1 + a_i/2?

In the change = a_j*(x-1) + a_i*(1/x - 1), and we want this < 0.

Since a_j ≥ 1, a_j*(x-1) ≥ x-1.

So if x-1 > a_i/2, then even with a_j=1, change ≥ x-1 - a_i/2 > 0.

So when x-1 > a_i/2, i.e., x > 1 + a_i/2, then change ≥ (x-1) - a_i/2 > 0.

Therefore, for x > 1 + a_i/2, change > 0, so it increases total.

So we can safely ignore such x.

For x ≤ 1 + a_i/2, change could be negative or positive, so we need to check.

But is there any x that satisfies x ≤ 1 + a_i/2 and gives negative change?

Yes, as in the example.

What about when x=1? We skip since x>1.

So only consider x>1 and x ≤ 1 + a_i/2.

Now, the total number of operations to try:
- For each i, number of divisors of a_i that are >1 and ≤ 1 + a_i/2.

Since a_i ≤ 100, and divisors are limited, and for each a_i, the number of divisors is small (≤12), and we only consider those ≤ 1 + a_i/2.

For example, a_i=100: divisors >1 and ≤ 1+50=51: all divisors except possibly large ones, but 100 has divisors 2,4,5,10,20,25,50. 50≤51, so all. 100/2=50, so x≤51, so all divisors >1 except 100 itself? x=100: 100 > 1+100/2=51, so x=100 is excluded.

So only x≤51, which is all divisors >1 and ≤51.

So only a few per a_i.

So total operations is O(n * d) where d is average number of divisors, which is small.

In worst case, d ≤ 12, so total operations ≤ 50000 * 12 = 600000, which is acceptable.

Now we need to precompute min_excl[i] for each i.

How to do this efficiently?

We can:
1. Create a list of all values with their frequencies
2. For each i, min_excl[i] = minimum of all a_j for j≠i

We can do this by:
- Find global_min = min(a)
- If global_min appears only once, then for any i where a[i] = global_min, min_excl[i] = second_min
- Otherwise, for all i, min_excl[i] = global_min

But we need to be careful.

Actually:
- If there are at least two machines with value equal to global_min, then for any i, min_excl[i] = global_min
- If there is only one machine with value global_min, then for that machine, min_excl[i] = second_min, for others min_excl[i] = global_min

So we can:
1. Count frequencies of each value
2. Find global_min and second_min
3. For each i:
   - if a[i] == global_min and freq[global_min] == 1:
       then min_excl[i] = second_min
   - else:
       min_excl[i] = global_min

We can compute this in O(n).

Here's the plan:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

original_sum = sum(a)

# Precompute min_excl[i] for each i
from collections import Counter
freq = Counter(a)
values = sorted(freq.keys())
global_min = values[0]
second_min = values[1] if len(values) > 1 else float('inf')

# If there's only one distinct value, then second_min is the same as global_min
# But we need second_min only if there are at least two distinct values

# Actually, if frequency of global_min is 1, we need second_min
# if frequency of global_min > 1, then second_min doesn't matter

# Find the minimum value excluding position i
min_excl = [0] * n
global_freq = freq[global_min]
if global_freq == 1:
    # Only one machine has global_min, so for that machine, min_excl[i] = second_min for that i
    # For others, min_excl[i] = global_min
    second_val = second_min
    for i in range(n):
        if a[i] == global_min:
            min_excl[i] = second_val
        else:
            min_excl[i] = global_min
else:
    # Multiple machines have global_min, so min_excl[i] = global_min for all i
    for i in range(n):
        min_excl[i] = global_min

# Now try all operations
min_total = original_sum  # at least the original sum

for i in range(n):
    # Find all divisors x > 1 of a[i] such that x <= 1 + a[i]/2
    a_i = a[i]
    # Find divisors of a_i
    divisors = []
    x = 2
    while x * x <= a_i:
        if a_i % x == 0:
            divisors.append(x)
            if x != a_i // x:
                divisors.append(a_i // x)
        x += 1
    
    # Also add divisors from the factorization, but we have all divisors
    # Now filter divisors that are >1 and <= 1 + a_i/2
    valid_x = []
    for x in divisors:
        if x > 1 and x <= 1 + a_i / 2:
            valid_x.append(x)
    
    for x in valid_x:
        # For this i and x, we can reduce a[i] by x, and increase some a[j] by x
        # To minimize the change, we take j with minimum a[j] (excluding i)
        min_other = min_excl[i]
        # Change in total = new_total - original_total
        # = (a_j * x - a_j) + (a_i/x - a_i) = a_j*(x-1) + a_i*(1/x - 1)
        change = min_other * (x - 1) + a_i * (1/x - 1)
        if change < 0:
            new_total = original_sum + change
            if new_total < min_total:
                min_total = new_total

print(min_total)
```

Let me test with the examples:

Example 1: [1,2,3,4,5]
original_sum = 15

For each i:
i=0: a[0]=1, divisors: only 1, so no x>1 → skip
i=1: a[1]=2, divisors: 2, check x=2: 2 <= 1 + 2/2 = 2 → yes
  valid_x=[2]
  min_excl[1]: global_min=1, freq[1]=1 (only at index 0), so for i=1≠0, min_excl[1]=1
  change = 1*(2-1) + 2*(1/2 - 1) = 1 + 2*(-0.5) = 1-1 = 0 → not <0 → skip
i=2: a[2]=3, divisors: 3, 1 → valid x=3? 3 <= 1+3/2=2.5? 3>2.5 → no → skip
i=3: a[3]=4, divisors: 2,4 → check x=2: 2<=1+4/2=3 → yes; x=4: 4<=3? no → so only x=2
  min_excl[3]: a[3]=4, not global_min (global_min=1, one occurrence), so min_excl[3]=1
  change = 1*(2-1) + 4*(1/2 - 1) = 1 + 4*(-0.5) = 1-2 = -1 < 0 → new_total = 15-1=14
  min_total = min(15,14)=14
i=4: a[4]=5, divisors: 5,1 → x=5? 5<=1+5/2=3.5? no → skip

So answer=14 ✓

Example 2: [4,2,4,4]
original_sum = 14

global_min=2, freq[2]=1 (only at index 1), freq[4]=3

min_excl:
- i=0: a[0]=4 ≠2 → min_excl[0]=2
- i=1: a[1]=2 → min_excl[1]=4 (since only one 2)
- i=2: a[2]=4 → min_excl[2]=2
- i=3: a[3]=4 → min_excl[3]=2

i=0: a[0]=4, divisors: 2,4 → x=2: 2<=1+4/2=3 → yes; x=4: 4<=3? no → only x=2
  change = min_excl[0]*(2-1) + 4*(1/2-1) = 2*(1) + 4*(-0.5) = 2-2=0 → skip
i=1: a[1]=2, divisors: 2; check x=2: 2<=1+2/2=2 → yes
  change = min_excl[1]*(2-1) + 2*(1/2-1) = 4*(1) + 2*(-0.5) = 4-1=3>0 → skip
i=2: a[2]=4, same as i=0 → only x=2
  change = min_excl[2]*(1) + 4*(-0.5) = 2 - 2 = 0 → skip
i=3: same → skip

So min_total=14 ✓

Example 3: [2,4,2,3,7]
original_sum = 2+4+2+3+7 = 18

global_min=2, freq[2]=2 (indices 0,2), so for i with a[i]=2, min_excl[i]=2 (since multiple 2s)
For others: min_excl[i]=2

i=0: a[0]=2, divisors: 2 → x=2: 2<=1+2/2=2 → yes
  change = min_excl[0]*(1) + 2*(1/2-1) = 2*(1) + 2*(-0.5) = 2-1=1>0 → skip
i=1: a[1]=4, divisors: 2,4 → x=2: 2<=3 → yes; x=4: 4<=3? no → only x=2
  change = min_excl[1]*(1) + 4*(-0.5) = 2 - 2 = 0 → skip
i=2: same as i=0 → change = 2 + 2*(-0.5)=1>0 → skip
i=3: a[3]=3, divisors: 3 → x=3: 3<=1+3/2=2.5? no → skip
i=4: a[4]=7, divisors: 7 → 7<=1+7/2=4.5? no → skip

So min_total=18 ✓

Perfect!

One more edge: what if a_i=1? Then no divisor x>1, so skip.

Also, what if a_i=6? divisors: 2,3,6 → x≤1+6/2=4 → so x=2,3
x=6>4 → skip

So it's correct.

Final code:

```python
n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

original_sum = sum(a)

from collections import Counter
freq = Counter(a)
values = sorted(freq.keys())
global_min = values[0]
if len(values) > 1:
    second_min = values[1]
else:
    second_min = float('inf')

# Precompute min_excl[i] for each i
min_excl = [0] * n
global_freq = freq[global_min]

if global_freq == 1:
    # Only one machine has global_min
    for i in range(n):
        if a[i] == global_min:
            min_excl[i] = second_min
        else:
            min_excl[i] = global_min
else:
    # Multiple machines have global_min, so minimum excluding any i is global_min
    for i in range(n):
        min_excl[i] = global_min

min_total = original_sum

# Try all possible operations
for i in range(n):
    a_i = a[i]
    # Find all divisors of a_i that are >1 and <= 1 + a_i/2
    divisors = []
    x = 2
    while x * x <= a_i:
        if a_i % x == 0:
            divisors.append(x)
            if x != a_i // x:
                divisors.append(a_i // x)
        x += 1
    
    for x in divisors:
        if x > 1 and x <= 1 + a_i / 2:
            min_other = min_excl[i]
            change = min_other * (x - 1) + a_i * (1/x - 1)
            if change < 0:
                new_total = original_sum + change
                if new_total < min_total:
                    min_total = new_total

print(min_total)
```

n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

original_sum = sum(a)

from collections import Counter
freq = Counter(a)
values = sorted(freq.keys())
global_min = values[0]
if len(values) > 1:
    second_min = values[1]
else:
    second_min = float('inf')

# Precompute min_excl[i] for each i
min_excl = [0] * n
global_freq = freq[global_min]

if global_freq == 1:
    # Only one machine has global_min
    for i in range(n):
        if a[i] == global_min:
            min_excl[i] = second_min
        else:
            min_excl[i] = global_min
else:
    # Multiple machines have global_min, so minimum excluding any i is global_min
    for i in range(n):
        min_excl[i] = global_min

min_total = original_sum

# Try all possible operations
for i in range(n):
    a_i = a[i]
    # Find all divisors of a_i that are >1 and <= 1 + a_i/2
    divisors = []
    x = 2
    while x * x <= a_i:
        if a_i % x == 0:
            divisors.append(x)
            if x != a_i // x:
                divisors.append(a_i // x)
        x += 1
    
    for x in divisors:
        if x > 1 and x <= 1 + a_i / 2:
            min_other = min_excl[i]
            change = min_other * (x - 1) + a_i * (1/x - 1)
            if change < 0:
                new_total = original_sum + change
                if new_total < min_total:
                    min_total = new_total

print(min_total)
